- **[SemantEq: Faithful Bidirectional Equivalence of Formal Statements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32919)**  `arXiv:2610.32919`  
  _Xinrui Li, Pawan Sasanka Ammanamanchi, Siddharth Bhat, Rianna Jamdar, Yongxi Lin, Stella Biderman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Statement autoformalization translates mathematics from natural language into a proof assistant such as Lean 4, and models now do it at scale. As provers get stronger, the burden of correctness shifts from the proof to the statement, that is, to whether the formal statement faithfully translates its source. Given a trusted reference formalization, the natural test is logical equivalence, and it proves too much. $P \leftrightarrow Q$ holds trivially for any two statements $P$ and $Q$ that the library proves, because a prover can prove each separately and pass through $P \leftrightarrow \mathrm{True} \leftrightarrow Q$. The stronger the prover, the more unrelated pairs pass. Earlier methods restrict the prover and check that each proof names the other statement. We introduce SemantEq, a more principled relation that instead requires each statement to turn into the other only through a chain of allowed steps, such as logical rearrangement and rewriting with the library's own equations. Our programs judge a proof by the proof term Lean checks, not by its tactic script. SemantEq-Eval, a search without a model, reaches 100% precision and 65.6% recall on the ProofNetVerif test split under our adjudicated labels, against 95.8% and 61.8% for BEq+, and an 8.9-fold increase in its CPU time adds at most three pairs. A language model finds more proofs, but under two of four prompts it proves both directions of about two in five of the non-equivalent pairs. SemantEq-ProofAudit audits each model-written proof under a policy derived from the relation and accepts none of the 104 non-equivalent pairs, and it raises recall on 407 equivalent pairs from 65.4% to 81.1% under the best prompt. Separating the generation of equivalence proofs from their acceptance lets a stronger prover raise recall without changing what an acceptance means. We release code and data at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Closing the Modality Gap via Simplex-Constrained Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32890)**  `arXiv:2610.32890`  
  _Shubham Gupta, Siva Reddy, Perouz Taslakian, Valentina Zantedeschi, Cem Subakan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal contrastive models like CLIP and CLAP successfully learn shared representations, their embeddings typically exhibit a persistent \emph{modality gap}. We propose addressing this separation through a purely geometric approach: changing the embedding domain from the unit sphere, $S^{d-1}$, to the probability simplex, $\Delta^{d-1}$. Although both manifolds share the same intrinsic dimension, the simplex acts as a structural prior; its nonnegative, unit-mass constraints serve as a cross-modal calibration that forces both modalities to ground themselves in the same coordinate system. By attaching lightweight softmax adapters and using Total Variation similarity, we reduce the centroid $\ell_2$ gap by 97--99\% across five multimodal retrieval benchmarks. This confirms that simplicial geometry induces genuine semantic alignment, matching or exceeding the retrieval performance of standard unconstrained models.
  </details>

- **[NgramRL: Accelerating RL Post-Training for Structured Generation via Rollout-Aware N-gram Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32881)**  `arXiv:2610.32881`  
  _Nilesh Prasad Pandey, Jangseon Park, Jason Kong, Onat Gungor, Tajana Rosing_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but its cost is dominated by autoregressive rollout generation, which can exceed 70% of training time. Existing speculative-decoding methods are poorly suited to this setting: model-based drafters consume scarce GPU memory and may become misaligned as the policy evolves, while existing model-free methods do not explicitly exploit repetition across training epochs. We observe that recent rollouts of the same prompt provide a high-quality, self-updating draft source. Based on this insight, we propose NgramRL, a per-prompt n-gram drafter constructed from bounded recent rollout history. NgramRL requires no auxiliary model, drafter training, or persistent GPU-resident drafter state, while target-model verification preserves the rollout distribution. To hide CPU cache lookup and scheduling, we further introduce an adaptive fan-out prefetcher that predicts likely verification outcomes (j, b), consisting of the accepted draft length j and target-emitted continuation token b. A causal budget allocator concentrates prefetches on high-probability outcomes, while uncovered outcomes fall back to sequential lookup without changing committed tokens. Across two model families and three reasoning and structured-generation workloads, NgramRL commits 3.60-7.77 tokens per target-model forward in the warm-cache regime, up to 1.99x more than SuffixDecoding and 7.0x more than prompt-lookup decoding. On Spider, the prefetcher covers 96.6% of Qwen3-8B outcomes at a candidate budget of 16 and improves measured rollout throughput by 1.38x over synchronous NgramRL, approaching its 1.40x overlap ceiling.
  </details>

- **[Advancing SVD-based LLM Compression via Layer-Wise Error Model Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32878)**  `arXiv:2610.32878`  
  _Moritz Thoma, Maximilian Groezinger, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Emad Aghajanzadeh, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Christos Anagnostopoulos, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank SVD-based compression offers a powerful strategy to reduce the computational costs of LLMs. However, existing methods face two key limitations: (i) global rank allocation, where uncalibrated error proxies fail to capture complex error propagation, and (ii) decomposition quality, where Fisher-based estimators suffer from severe rank collapse. In this work, we address these limitations by introducing Layer-wise Error Modeling Search (LEMS) and KFAC-SVD. LEMS advances rank allocation by introducing a layer-wise error surrogate that integrates local and global layer importance alongside a propagation bias, enabling effective global rank allocation via an ILP formulation. KFAC-SVD improves decomposition quality by utilizing token-wise statistics, mitigating the rank deficiency observed in prior Fisher-based SVD approaches. Across Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 model families, we show that LEMS consistently outperforms existing search strategies, delivering significant zero-shot accuracy gains of up to 4.8 p.p. that generalize to model sizes of 70B parameters, while KFAC-SVD achieves an average perplexity improvement of 15%. Project page and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[From Deliberately Constructed Systemic Representations of the World of Activity to Dialogue: A Five-Level Communication Architecture for Strong Language-Based ASI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32869)**  `arXiv:2610.32869`  
  _Maxim Osovsky_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper proposes a five-level organization of message formation and understanding within thinking and activity in which ASI participates. Its central thesis is that a language model's context is fundamentally incapable of presenting the foundations of utterance formation for examination and verification. These foundations are established in advance by acquired schemas, systemic representations of the world of activity, and connections accepted as true within them. Neither context nor trained LLM weights, in themselves, attest to the presence of deliberately constructed systemic representations. The five levels distinguish systemic representations of the world of activity, a particular situation cut out from those representations, its narrative description, the full sense of a message, and text. A situation's schema-model is not identical to a story about it. A recipient reconstructs the full sense from an abbreviated text through jointly constructed and agreed-upon representations and joint activity. Reverse work proceeds from the full sense through an extended description and the situation's schema-model to the reconstruction of systemic foundations. The theoretical foundations draw on the systems methodology of G. P. Shchedrovitskiy, the structural differential of A. Korzybski, and the Meaning-Text theory of I. A. Mel'chuk. The contribution is a specific systemic-activity organization of content for examining and adjusting message foundations and controlling the consequences of their transformation. Further research concerns large graphical models of activity on reconstructible systemic foundations, distinct from text generation from context.
  </details>

- **[A Foundation Model for Policy In-Context Longitudinal Causal Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32863)**  `arXiv:2610.32863`  
  _Wenxin Chen, Kyra Gan, Mingyu Lu, Zilin Jing, Fei Wang, Hoifung Poon, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Longitudinal causal effect estimation is complicated by time-varying confounding and treatment-confounder feedback loops. Existing methods generally require data-specific nuisance-model fitting, and many are tailored to prespecified treatment sequences or prediction horizons, leading to time-consuming computation at deployment. We introduce *Policy In-Context Longitudinal causal Effect estimation with Prior-data Fitted Networks* (**PICLE-PFN**), the first foundation model for longitudinal causal effect estimation that supports *dynamic* treatment policies. PICLE-PFN represents target policies through in-context history-decision demonstrations, providing a unified interface for a broad class of treatment policies. Pretrained over synthetic data sampled from longitudinal structural causal models, PICLE-PFN amortizes causal effect estimation across datasets, policies, and horizons. Given a new observational dataset and target policy, it directly predicts conditional average potential outcomes (CAPOs) in one forward pass without task-specific training or hyperparameter tuning. Across synthetic and semi-synthetic longitudinal benchmarks, PICLE-PFN achieves competitive RMSE and substantially improved uncertainty calibration with a fraction of deployment runtime, compared to state-of-the-art conventional estimators trained and tuned separately for each dataset.
  </details>

- **[Reimagining Enterprise RAG for Multi-Agent Systems: A Knowledge Layer That Organizes Before It Retrieves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32851)**  `arXiv:2610.32851`  
  _Garima Agrawal, Prasun Das, Akshay Sarvade, Priyanka L, Minisha N, Sravani Joshna, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprises are deploying agentic AI across the software development lifecycle, and they need the behavioral logic of the systems they act on, not stakeholder documentation. A seven-agent reverse-engineering pipeline recovered this logic, processing 241 repositories across 18 services into thousands of artifacts describing scope, journeys, rules and acceptance criteria. Recovery does not confer usability. Entities like offer, eligibility, and activation recur across services under a shared vocabulary, related in name but operationally distinct, so one end-to-end journey implicates over a thousand near-identical rules that must not be conflated. Flat vector retrieval fails, returning an identical rule from the wrong service at high similarity with no signal of error. Content-level ontology, the traditional remedy, did not exist, and relating every occurrence of recurring entities would have cost more than the engagement and not survived a release cycle. We place the ontology at the artifact level. In this machine-generated corpus, each artifact retains its producer, component, service, domain and type, provenance that disambiguates before content is read. We present a knowledge layer that organizes before retrieving: a self-describing catalog, a sparse metadata registry, and a navigation hypergraph defined over artifacts rather than extracted entities. Queries resolve to artifact identifiers that constrain vector search, so the competing rule from another service is absent rather than outranked, making disambiguation structural, not statistical. Built in under a week and admitting new services by convention, the layer serves SDLC agents, developers, quality engineers and analysts. It supported root-cause analysis and resolution of 100+ production defects with developer verification and attributed 35 release regression failures to specific rules and services.
  </details>

- **[HarnessMM: Recursive Self-Improvement for Harness Token Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32813)**  `arXiv:2610.32813`  
  _Yuhua Jiang, Chang Xu, Feifei Gao, Li Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses repeatedly invoke reasoning after tool feedback, even when an earlier call has already fixed the next action. We introduce HarnessMM (Harness Minus Minus), a method for improving harness token efficiency through guarded decision reuse and recursive self-improvement (RSI). RSI iteratively refines companion harness configurations from training feedback, with validation selecting the configuration for deployment. A controller at the model-tool boundary reuses resolved decisions to replace later reasoning callbacks while actor weights remain fixed. Executable Decision Graphs store exact actions, literal arguments, causal dependencies, and public guards. Guarded Decision Reuse activates these persistent decisions from observed receipts; Certified Callback Substitution executes them through the native interface and records removal of the registered slow callback. New semantic decisions, unsupported evidence, and selector abstention invoke the original actor again. The representation restricts what feedback may change, while complete-task measurements establish quality and cost. On HumanEval, HarnessMM preserves Codex success at 90.82\% while reducing Tokens/task (k) from 66.72 to 47.02 (29.54\% saving). For DeepSeek Harness, success rises from 71.43\% to 85.71\% while Tokens/task (k) fall from 96.28 to 11.62 (87.93\% saving). A separate MBPP ablation measures 26.91\% lower cost than single-use graphs and 19.52\% lower cost than retained callbacks. These results establish guarded decision reuse as a practical way to reduce repeated reasoning in existing harnesses.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Hazard Mapper: Emulating Expert Map-Making with AI Agents for Risk Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32738)**  `arXiv:2610.32738`  
  _Sidi Wu, Lifeng Lin, Yizi Chen, Anna Yokokubo, Lorenz Hurni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hazard maps are essential tools for public risk communication, preparedness, and evacuation planning, yet effective cartographic design is not always ensured due to limited access to cartographic expertise across local governments and communities. We present Agentic Hazard Mapper, a multi-agent system that automates hazard-map production by emulating professional cartographic workflows and iterative design trade-offs while adapting design decisions to different data, locations, and hazard contexts. We conducted a formative study with map experts to inform the system design, followed by a user study and interviews with map experts and hazard practitioners. The generated maps improved participants' spatial understanding and evacuation-planning performance, reduced perceived workload and task completion time, and were generally preferred over corresponding municipal maps. We further discuss human oversight, adaptation across users and communication contexts, and opportunities to extend agentic support across broader stages of hazard-map production and risk communication.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Trust Their Own Justifications? A Study on Functional Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32665)**  `arXiv:2610.32665`  
  _Alisson Rosa Pereira, Bruno Iochins Grisci_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have been widely adopted in text classification tasks, where they not only output class predictions but also generate explanations that highlight the tokens deemed most relevant for reaching the predicted label. Yet it remains unclear whether these highlighted elements are behaviorally consistent with the model's own predictions under targeted interventions, which we refer to as functional auto-consistency. While much of the literature evaluates the textual plausibility of such explanations, few studies assess their functional consistency with the model's actual behavior. In this work, we propose an experimental framework based on the principle of auto-consistency: if a model identifies certain tokens as decisive, then isolating, removing, or semantically inverting them should produce systematic and interpretable changes in its predictions. We operationalize this evaluation through sufficiency, comprehensiveness, and counterfactuality metrics, and conduct experiments on IMDB and Steam reviews across both closed-source (GPT-4o-mini) and open-source LLMs (Gemma3, Granite8B, DeepSeek). Results show that GPT-4o-mini follows the expected progression across all metrics, Gemma3 and Granite8B maintain coherence under sufficiency but lose consistency under more demanding interventions, while DeepSeek variants display structural deviations, either failing to preserve sufficiency or overreacting under comprehensiveness and counterfactuality. These findings show that explanation reliability varies across LLM families and scales, with smaller models displaying contradictions and larger ones exhibiting over-sensitivity. By combining sufficiency, comprehensiveness, and counterfactuality, our approach provides a systematic methodology for assessing the functional consistency of LLM self-explanations.
  </details>

- **[WiMoT: Replay-Free Continual Wi-Fi Sensing via Moment Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32660)**  `arXiv:2610.32660`  
  _Chunchun Yang, Yang Cao, Zhitang Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual Wi-Fi sensing must balance plasticity, learning new rooms or users, against stability, keeping earlier ones, yet nodes often cannot retain channel state information (CSI) for privacy and storage reasons. We propose WiMoT (Wi-Fi Moment Transport), which stores the per-class feature mean and covariance of every earlier domain instead of samples. When the representation changes, stored statistics become stale. WiMoT restricts the change to an affine map on a frozen feature basis and applies the same map to the statistics, so they stay valid without old samples. Supervised contrastive learning learns the new domain, a closed-form loss on the stored statistics keeps earlier classes separable, and a Gaussian discriminant classifier, refitted from the transported statistics with equal weight per domain, predicts without domain identity. On 3DO, WiMANS, and Widar3.0, WiMoT's average final accuracies are 94.02%, 89.61%, and 95.16% with at most 0.58 percentage points (pp) of forgetting. On 3DO and Widar3.0, it also has the highest balance of new- and earlier-domain accuracy and is 3.77 and 13.11 pp ahead of the strongest of 21 baselines, which uses replay, staying ahead across domain orders and label budgets. On WiMANS, it matches the strongest replay baseline in accuracy (+0.41 pp) while reducing forgetting from 2.78 to 0.00 pp, and has the highest six-order average accuracy. Because full covariances dominate the memory, storing them as rank-32 factors shrinks the moment memory to 6.4%, losing at most 0.49 pp of accuracy, within edge-node storage budgets.
  </details>

- **[LGMT: A Tool for Testing the Logical Reasoning Reliability of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32627)**  `arXiv:2610.32627`  
  _Zenghui Zhou, Xiaoke Fang, Zheng Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly serve as reasoning components in modern software systems, yet a model that answers a logical query correctly can contradict itself when the same problem is restated in an equivalent form. Static benchmarks evaluate queries in isolation and therefore fail to expose such inconsistencies. To address this limitation, we present LGMT, an open-source tool that detects reasoning inconsistencies through metamorphic relations grounded in first-order logic. Given a source test case in premise-conclusion form, LGMT generates logically equivalent follow-up cases and checks whether the model's entailment predictions agree across the pair, without ground-truth labels. The tool provides 20 built-in metamorphic relations, supports custom ones verified by the Z3 theorem prover, and runs from a CLI or a web studio, including an offline mock mode requiring no API key. Across six widely used LLMs on a bundled benchmark of 3,091 metamorphic groups, LGMT reveals inconsistency rates ranging from 20.84% to 29.44%. LGMT is publicly available on GitHub atthis https URL, with a demonstration video atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LLaCO: Continuous Diffusion for Large Language Models with Competition-Aware Semantic Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32604)**  `arXiv:2610.32604`  
  _Zijian Jia, Peixuan Zhang, Genying Li, Di Wu, Yujie Fu, Si Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous diffusion models have emerged as a promising non-autoregressive paradigm for text generation over continuous embedding spaces. Along the continuous trajectory, the residual token identity directly shapes the denoising objective. However, the geometric trajectory misaligns with temporal progression, triggering severe temporal compression. In this paper, we show that embedding norm decay causes Euclidean distance to yield spurious overtaking, indicating that token competition must be evaluated directionally. In this space, a candidate overtakes the source token only when the effective noise magnitude surpasses their semantic separation. We formalize these pairwise dynamics as the Competition Threshold Theory (CTT). Crucially, high-dimensional overtaking requires extreme noise levels, causing standard trajectories to concentrate identity decay into a narrow window near the noisy endpoint. To resolve this, we propose Semantic Retention Scheduling (SRS) to align time allocation with the progression of identity decay, and demonstrate that existing token identity metrics are fundamentally statistical aggregations of these pairwise crossings. Building on these theoretical insights, we introduce LLaCO, a generative framework powered by competition-aware semantic scheduling. LLaCO adapts a pretrained backbone to continuous representations via an embedding directional adapter and timestep-conditioned modulation. To operate within the directional space, LLaCO employs a spherical noising process formulated as a geodesic interpolation toward a mask-biased endpoint, eliminating norm decay while preserving accurate token competition. Guided by SRS, LLaCO reallocates the denoising trajectory proportionally to the logarithmic rank decay. Instantiated as an 8B model, LLaCO outperforms previous continuous diffusion baselines and rivals discrete diffusion models of comparable scale.
  </details>

- **[IGG: A Benchmark for Interactive GUI Grounding under Visibility Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32536)**  `arXiv:2610.32536`  
  _Kyeongseon Kim, Jiyeon Son, Tae-Hyun Oh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding benchmarks ask whether an agent can localize a target that is already visible in a static screenshot. Real interfaces often violate this assumption: a target may be off-screen, appear only after a state change or hover, remain occluded, or become actionable after a delay. This leaves the ability to recover target visibility unevaluated. To measure this reveal step, we introduce Interactive GUI Grounding (IGG), a benchmark in which an agent must first make the target visible through interaction and then click it. IGG contains 601 tasks across 16 controllable GUI environments and seven visibility-constraint sub-types, with a compact action space of scrolling, moving, dragging, waiting, and clicking. In our human study, participants achieve 94.6% accuracy, while GPT-5.5 reaches 79.4% and the strongest evaluated open-source VLM reaches only 31.6%. To identify the source of this gap, we conduct two controlled interventions. Telling Qwen3-VL which interaction to perform raises its accuracy only from 30.8% to 33.9%, whereas performing the interaction for the model raises it to 81.9%. The bottleneck is executing the reveal interaction, not recognizing the target once it is exposed. We further show that this capability is learnable, demonstrating that visibility recovery can be strengthened consistently across model families.
  </details>

- **[CASE: Confusion-Aware Skill Evolution for Frozen Multimodal Emotion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32481)**  `arXiv:2610.32481`  
  _Siyu Wu, Zeyu Liang, Linghao Wang, Qiyun Zheng, Yi Yao, Ling Lo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially advanced Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) by enabling fine-grained emotion understanding through reasoning over multimodal evidence. However, they still exhibit systematic confusion between semantically adjacent emotions with highly overlapping multimodal cues. Existing approaches typically address these errors through global parameter adaptation, making confusion correction costly and inflexible. We instead observe that emotion confusion is highly localized and therefore better addressed through reusable pair-specific expertise. Building on this insight, we propose Confusion-Aware Skill Evolution (CASE), the first agent-skill framework for MER. CASE maintains a sparse, self-evolving library of pair-specific EmotionSkills that externalize affective expertise from model parameters, enabling the learned expertise to be continuously refined and readily transferred across diverse frozen MLLM backbones. During inference, CASE dynamically retrieves the appropriate EmotionSkill to reinforce boundary-aware reasoning while keeping the MLLM backbone frozen. Extensive experiments on CA-MER, DFEW, and EMER demonstrate the effectiveness and transferability of CASE across both general-purpose and emotion-specialized MLLMs. Notably, CASE improves the average accuracy of Qwen3-VL-8B on the CA-MER benchmark from 46.80% to 59.13%, while achieving consistent gains across MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477)**  `arXiv:2610.32477`  
  _Kaiwen Wu, Chaochao Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[ALLMTS: Automated LLM-based Medical Triage System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32469)**  `arXiv:2610.32469`  
  _Michał Remigiusz Janiszewski, Artur Stopa, Łukasz Lenkiewicz, Wojciech Achtelik, Bartosz Gonczarek_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Telephone triage is a safety critical task: from a brief intake note, a nurse must decide whether a caller needs an ambulance, an emergency department, an appointment, or self care at home. Queried directly, large language models (LLMs) are unreliable: they over-escalate routine cases and cannot justify their recommendations against an auditable clinical standard. We present the Automated LLM-based Medical Triage System (ALLMTS), which grounds every disposition in the same commercially maintained telehealth guidelines nurses use. Rather than asking an LLM to triage, ALLMTS makes it execute the guideline: it selects the appropriate guideline and evaluates its triage assessment questions. The disposition follows deterministically from the first positive answer, using the same traversal rule a nurse follows. We evaluate ALLMTS on 566 synthetic, expert-validated scenarios spanning 14 dispositions and designed to test specific guideline questions. ALLMTS achieves 0.95 accuracy, compared with 0.47 for a raw-LLM baseline. On a curated 50-scenario subset released as an open benchmark, ALLMTS reaches 0.98 accuracy, on par with the 0.944 correct disposition rate that a five nurse expert panel recorded on those same scenarios. Critically, on that subset the baselines have over-triage rates of up to 0.50 (0.44 on the full dataset), whereas ALLMTS reduces the over-triage rate to at most 0.02 while maintaining low under-triage. These results support coupling LLM language understanding with executable clinical guidelines for triage from intake notes.
  </details>

- **[POSEIDON: Scaling Training Environments for Scientific Agents from Code Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32448)**  `arXiv:2610.32448`  
  _Qifan Zhang, Yuhan Li, Nuo Chen, Bowen Liu, Xiao Hu, Miao Peng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training environments remain a major bottleneck for agents in data-driven scientific discovery. Existing collections of executable and verifiable training environments are small, and their construction remains difficult to scale. To address this limitation, we introduce Poseidon, an automated pipeline for generating diverse, verifiable training environments from scientific code repositories at scale. From the same repository budget, Poseidon extracts nearly 10 times as many training environments as a prior approach by decomposing scientific workflows into four complementary task types: Reproduction, Subflow, Completion, and Correction, where each type targets a distinct capability for scientific research. A recursive improvement loop further refines failed candidates and recovers otherwise discarded environments. Using Poseidon, we construct Pearl, a collection of 97,659 executable and verifiable environments from 7,110 public repositories spanning 10 scientific disciplines. To the best of our knowledge, Pearl is the largest and most broadly multidisciplinary collection of scientific-agent training environments to date. Qwen3.5 models (9B, 27B, and 35B-A3B) trained on Pearl achieve consistent gains across six challenging scientific-agent benchmarks. Overall, the scale and diversity of Pearl offer opportunities for training scientific discovery agents.
  </details>

- **[When to Stop Penalizing Actions? Martingale GRPO for VLA Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32431)**  `arXiv:2610.32431`  
  _Zongyao Zhao, Cheng-Jie Zhao, Ruihang Jiang, Bing Zheng, Zhaolin Wang, Yuanwei Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A failed vision-language-action (VLA) rollout does not imply that every action contributed to failure. Yet in group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), a single trajectory-level advantage is assigned to all actions in a rollout. Potentially useful recovery attempts can thus be penalized alongside the errors they aim to correct. Ideal stopping conditions for failure feedback are characterized using success-probability and policy-score martingales. At these stops, the removed gradient contribution has zero conditional expectation given the retained information. Truncation therefore preserves the expectation of a reference GRPO group-gradient estimator and can reduce its covariance. One such stop occurs when the conditional probability of success is zero, motivating error and recovery cues for selecting candidate feedback boundaries. Martingale GRPO (MART-GRPO) is introduced with a vision-language model (VLM) for event-guided truncation. The VLM is fine-tuned on our annotated training set using low-rank adaptation (LoRA). The last visible error without observed recovery is localized by the frozen VLM in complete rollout videos. Failure feedback is retained through the selected error interval and removed afterward, while successful-rollout loss terms and original loss normalization are preserved. For retrospective boundaries, changes in expectation and variance are analyzed to assess departures from ideal stopping. Experiments with OpenVLA-OFT on RoboTwin 2.0 show that MART-GRPO improves success rates over full-trajectory GRPO across multiple tasks.
  </details>

- **[Async-RBench: Benchmarking Asynchronous Task Execution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32429)**  `arXiv:2610.32429`  
  _Detao Zeng, Guilin Ou, Nuo Chen, Yunhao Feng, Hongliang Wang, Qinli Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute tasks asynchronously: a main agent continues working while its subagents run, and the task state may change before their results arrive. The main agent must decide which work to update or preserve and which checks to repeat, while avoiding prohibited changes. Final task success alone does not reveal whether these results were handled correctly, and natural asynchronous arrivals are hard to reproduce. We introduce Async-RBench, a benchmark for asynchronous task execution in LLM agents, with 200 executable tasks covering eight scenarios. A scenario controller regulates result delivery using predefined task-state conditions, while an Event Response Contract (ERC) specifies what each response must change, preserve, avoid, and re-verify. The Dynamic Replanning Score (DRS) weights the response process and outcome equally and is reported separately from task success. We evaluate nine agents and find DRS scores of 19.2 to 45.1, with the lowest scores on duplicate reports and subagent failures. Under our execution protocol, all nine agents obtain lower task success with asynchronous than with batched delivery (26.0% vs. 29.7% on average). For three agents, prompting the main agent to rebuild its plan after every result improves DRS by only 2.6 to 3.5 points. Together, these findings highlight the challenges of reliable asynchronous task execution and the need to evaluate how agents handle incoming results alongside end-to-end task success.
  </details>

- **[LeafSkill: Efficient Leaf-Level Action Grounding for Recursive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32412)**  `arXiv:2610.32412`  
  _Shen Liu, Ze Zhou, Zhenguo Xu, Shaopu Wang, Yike Gao, Chunlei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive large language model (LLM) agents solve long-horizon tasks through decomposition, execution, and environmental feedback. Yet after producing an atomic subtask, they often invoke the same large model again to translate a familiar instruction into an executable action. We observe that high-level planning is task-specific, whereas leaf-level action grounding recurs across tasks and can be checked against environment-admitted actions. We introduce LeafSkill, an external reuse layer that retrieves semantically related grounding experience, binds action templates to current objects and admissible actions, and uses a lightweight 1.7B selector to choose a bound action or defer the unchanged subtask to the original agent. Skills remain outside the model parameters, allowing a frozen selector to use post-training additions. Under lexical and syntactic paraphrases, exact matching retrieves none of the reference actions, while semantic retrieval preserves 96.0% and 97.0% candidate coverage; the frozen selector adopts 97.34% of unseen skill types. On an independent confirmation set of 200 Robotouille tasks, LeafSkill completes 137 tasks versus 121 for ReCAP (68.5% vs. 60.5%, exact McNemar p=.0259). On the 106 tasks completed by both, it reduces Qwen3.5-27B tokens by 14.42% and calls by 14.08%. A separate held-out repeat yields a consistent 9.0-point success gain and 18.98% token reduction. Reusable leaf grounding can thus improve task completion while reducing host-model inference without replacing high-level planning.
  </details>

- **[DealsFlow: An Evidence-Gated Agentic Harness for Venture Capital Deal Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32380)**  `arXiv:2610.32380`  
  _Ming Ze Tang, David Cutting, Boyd Carson, Vasiliki Carson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can read startup pitch decks, but letting them emit investment scores directly invites score inflation, run-to-run variance and rationales that are hard to audit. We describe DealsFlow, a human-in-the-loop agentic harness deployed at a UK early-stage fund. The LLM reads the deck, cites evidence and rates each category; code combines the ratings, caps them and only ever lowers them. Three mechanisms implement this: a stage-aware corroboration ceiling (78, 70 or 60 out of 100) on factual categories without external evidence; a sceptic call that may lower but never raise a score; and a pre-fetch pattern that retrieves and indexes all evidence before a single generation pass. We evaluate on $N=243$ SEIS/EIS investments with recorded outcomes. Because recent investments have unresolved outcomes, we use the 142 that are at least three years old. There, the full pipeline's score has Spearman $\rho=0.45$ (95% CI 0.32 to 0.57) with the total-value multiple and AUC 0.71 for identifying write-offs, against $\rho=0.38$ and AUC 0.68 for an unconstrained LLM. The gain is small and comes mostly from the sceptic call; we did not test whether its lower-only rule matters. Investments that later exceeded $2\times$ had 64% of factual categories corroborated, against 38% for write-offs (omnibus Kruskal-Wallis test over five outcome groups, $p=0.031$), and our retrieval was not date-restricted, so these figures may overstate screening skill. In a study of 20 inbound applications, analyst time per application fell from 44.7 to 18.9 minutes including verification and correction, and 84% of 240 cited claims were fully supported by their sources, but 70% of reports needed at least one correction.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Control of Scientific Instruments with Nonstationary Responses through Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32370)**  `arXiv:2610.32370`  
  _Guoyang Shi, Hongwen Li, Yiding Yu, Zhuang Fu, Siqi Ding, Zhengyuan Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controlling scientific instruments requires parameters to be adjusted progressively in response to feedback from successive operations. When unobserved operating conditions evolve with operational history, responses become nonstationary, and a static mapping from actions to responses becomes inadequate for decisions across shots. Shot-to-shot tuning of neutral beam injection (NBI) ion sources in magnetic confinement fusion devices is representative of this class of problems: the conditions of electrode surfaces and vacuum are difficult to observe directly, discharge outcomes vary between shots, and experiments are costly and carry operational risks. We propose a physically grounded multimodal agent with three independently auditable components. Deterministic algorithms extract symbolic physical quantities and visual evidence from multichannel waveforms; a memory with two layers organizes trajectories from the current task, experience from earlier tasks, and procedural knowledge, from which a multimodal large language model (MLLM) generates candidate actions; and an independent action feasibility check enforces action constraints before execution. In a strictly chronological replay of 455 shots from four EXL-50U platforms, physical symbols alone outperformed waveform images alone by 0.21-0.32 in macro-F1 for quality classification across all four evaluated models. Retrieved context increased the mean agreement with historical action directions by 22.4-42.7 percentage points, whereas the contribution of visual evidence varied across models. Under supervised operation on five ion sources, the system achieved a beam-extraction rate of 86.80% across 409 executed shots. These results demonstrate the engineering feasibility of the framework for supervised sequential tuning on a physical scientific instrument.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: A Lightweight Mamba-2 Model for EEG-Based Cognitive Workload Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32361)**  `arXiv:2610.32361`  
  _Ioannis Kyprakis, Stefanos Gkikas, Chariklia Chatzaki, Zongyi Jiang, Randy Gomez, Raul Fernandez Rojas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cognitive workload estimation from physiological signals is increasingly relevant in high-risk environments, where continuous and unobtrusive monitoring can inform adaptive systems and safety interventions. This paper presents FOCUS (Frequency-band Optimized Cognitive-load Ultralight SSM), a compact Mamba-2-based model for EEG-based binary workload classification under constrained data conditions. Rather than learning frequency sensitivity from data, FOCUS encodes preprocessed multi-channel EEG using a fixed FFT decomposition that covers the five canonical EEG bands. The proposed model combines depthwise-separable convolution and a weight-shared encoder-bottleneck Mamba-2 pair, with 0.04 million parameters and 0.11 GFLOPs. Evaluated in a pilot study with 11 participants under leave-one-subject-out cross-validation across three cognitively distinct tasks, FOCUS achieves 74.75% accuracy on the full dataset at 0.08 ms GPU model-forward latency. In the weight-sharing ablation, the shared configuration achieves 4.55 percentage points higher accuracy on the full dataset than the non-shared variant while reducing the SSM parameter budget; however, this difference does not reach statistical significance under a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test ($p=0.083$). Overall, the selected FOCUS configuration provides a favorable trade-off between classification performance and computational cost within this controlled pilot setting.
  </details>

- **[TGR-EvoDAG: A DAG-based Multi-Agent Code Evolution Framework for Generative Recommendation on Tencent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32326)**  `arXiv:2610.32326`  
  _Junlin He, Junzhang Zhu, Yudong Li, Yihong Tang, Xuefeng Jiang, Wei Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents have enabled automated code evolution for algorithm discovery. However, current recursive self-improvement (RSI) research for sequential recommendation faces a dilemma: general-purpose frameworks lack domain-specific feedback, while recommender-specific frameworks rely on linear evolution. Crucially, naively integrating them fails when applied to Generative Recommendation (GR). Unlike end-to-end models, GR relies on a module-cascaded pipeline (tokenization, pre-training, and alignment). In this paradigm, existing evolution methods suffer from: i) cost blindness, squandering budgets by ignoring stage-asymmetric retraining overheads; and ii) short-sighted evaluation, discarding promising upstream innovations that temporarily regress metrics before downstream adaptation. To bridge these gaps, we propose TGR-EvoDAG, a multi-agent code evolution framework tailored for GR. TGR-EvoDAG models code evolution as a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) to maintain multiple evolution branches and merge complementary innovations, unifying advanced search with rich GR diagnostic feedback. We further introduce stage-aware cost planning to optimize GPU-hour budgets, and active downstream co-adaptation to navigate temporary regressions and unlock cross-stage gains. On public Amazon datasets, TGR-EvoDAG enables the first autonomous evolution of a complete GR pipeline, improving offline metrics by up to 37.4% and Agent4Rec simulated user satisfaction by 19.7% over the strongest baseline. Furthermore, in industrial deployment on Tencent's large-scale commercial media platform, the evolved GR achieves a 12.16% relative improvement in offline Hit@1 and a statistically significant 0.40% increase in online click-through rate (CTR).
  </details>

- **[From Visual Affinity to Learning Credit in Multimodal Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32324)**  `arXiv:2610.32324`  
  _Yuhang Han, Dingning Liu, Yujie Chen, Yaojie Zhang, Jingwei Song, Zequn Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves multimodal reasoning, but uniformly assigning a single response-level advantage to all tokens overlooks the distinct roles of visual observations and subsequent reasoning. Visual affinity alone is insufficient: predictive states may align with both image and prompt-context representations, and upweighting valid visual observations can amplify their negative-advantage updates when later reasoning fails. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{Outcome-Conditioned Visual Credit (OVC)}, a plug-in framework that separates visual-association measurement from token-level credit assignment and is adaptable to policy-optimization methods with uniform response-level advantage allocation. Relative Visual Encoding constructs a context-relative visual coordinate by contrasting each predictive state's affinity to image and prompt-context representations. Outcome-conditioned Evidence Calibration combines this coordinate with uncertainty-scaled predictive features and calibrates their contributions to outcome-discriminative evidence through mean contrasts between positive- and negative-advantage responses, scaled by regularized within-side variation. Visual association thus informs credit through observed outcome differences rather than directly determining update priority. Normalized Credit Allocation conditions the calibrated evidence on the response's advantage sign to produce positive token weights with unit global mean. These detached weights preserve advantage signs while modulating individual token-gradient contributions. OVC reuses existing rollouts without auxiliary reward models, process annotations, or inference-time modules. Instantiated through GSPO-token, OVC retains sequence-level clipping and consistently improves six-benchmark mean accuracy over GSPO across three models, with gains of 1.86-2.39 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Gain: Measuring Skill Internalization for Agent Skill Retirement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32322)**  `arXiv:2610.32322`  
  _Yanze Wang, Pengfei Yao, Tianyi Sun, Hongda Chen, Yunyun Han, Jun Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents retrieve skills from public hubs that hold up to about 100,000 entries and serve many models. As models improve, a hub must find the skills a given model has internalized, meaning it can do without them, and retire them for that model, yet internalization has not been measured with known reliability. Published rules that compare runs with and without a skill retire on the gain alone. The gain is near zero both for an internalized skill and when neither the model nor the skill delivers the capability, a state we call uncovered. We instead withhold the skill, score the model on a task synthesized from it, and trust a high unaided score only when another model gains from the skill, since nearly half of high scores come from tasks no model benefits from. Under four models, a published gain rule applied with one task per skill removes over half of the uncovered skills. On SkillsBench, 28 of its 31 retirements rest on probes no run passed in full, while retiring on the unaided score costs no detectable reward. Unaided verdicts reproduce across tasks more often than the gain rule's (Cohen's kappa 0.42 against 0.23) but differ across models, so a hub must read them per model. We release 1,251 skills with tasks and item scores under four models.
  </details>

- **[Solvent: Long-Horizon Reasoning for Multi-Step Retrosynthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32308)**  `arXiv:2610.32308`  
  _Yifei Wang, Botian Wang, YiFei Wang, Jiangtao Feng, Xinbo Zhang, Yawen Ouyang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step retrosynthesis requires recursively transforming a target molecule through successive reaction steps into purchasable building blocks, and recent work has begun to draw on large language model (LLM) reasoning to tackle the task. In its latest evolution, LLM reasoning has entered the long-horizon regime: models pursue objectives across interdependent steps, interact with environments, and adapt to feedback. This shift has markedly advanced LLM capabilities, as illustrated by breakthroughs in AI-driven mathematical research. At this frontier, we introduce Solvent, an 8B LLM that successfully extends long-horizon reasoning to multi-step retrosynthesis. Through a two-round post-training curriculum, Solvent learns to interleave iterative synthesis planning, external availability verification, and adaptive route revision. Experiments show that, following a $2.1\times$ performance boost from RL, Solvent scores 61.86% across four benchmarks, surpassing mid-scale language models and prior search-based retrosynthesis systems, and rivaling frontier trillion-parameter LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Lifelong Context Adaptation through Bidirectional Memory-Skill Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32295)**  `arXiv:2610.32295`  
  _Tianyi Xu, Huazheng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lifelong LLM agents must learn from an expanding history of interactions while making each decision within a bounded context window. Memory makes past experience available, but the agent may still need to infer how to solve each new task from the retrieved records. Skills provide reusable procedures, yet may omit the task-specific facts needed to apply them. Because retrieved memory and skills shape the agent's behavior, they also influence the experience available for future learning. We introduce MemCryst, a framework for lifelong context adaptation through bidirectional learning of memory and skills with a frozen language model. Recurring episodes stored in memory support skill creation and revision, and the interactions guided by those skills supply new memory for subsequent learning. Facts retrieved from memory and learned skills jointly form the agent's context, while task outcomes adjust the future retrieval influence of the records actually served without rewriting their content. Across ten settings spanning embodied control, long-document reading, conversation, mathematics, and code, MemCryst leads seven reported comparisons. On ALFWorld, joint serving achieves 63.17% success, compared with 59.83% for memory-only and 50.50% for skill-only serving. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Trial by Trace: Benchmarking AI Agent Harnesses for Reliability and Failure Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32285)**  `arXiv:2610.32285`  
  _Zhiyong Cao, Hengjie Zhu, Yongxin Wang, Xuchen Li, Jintao Li, Shuxia Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly used to solve real-world problems, and their effectiveness depends on both the underlying models and the harnesses that support their operation. However, most existing benchmarks focus on model capabilities and overall agent performance, with limited attention to the evaluation of agent harnesses. To address this gap, we introduce TraceTrial-Bench, which organizes harness reliability into seven components covering context, execution, governance, lifecycle, observability, tools, and verification. We construct 95 expert-level, mechanism-oriented tasks with executable or state-based ground truth to support semantically equivalent comparisons while preserving the native behavior of each harness. We design a Trajectory Tracing (TT) module to capture agent execution traces and introduce a two-layer evaluation framework that separately assesses agent performance and harness reliability. In addition, we design a Multi-source Evidence Acquisition (MEA) module to provide evidence from the agent context, harness, and execution environment for LLM-based evaluation, and develop a Failure Attribution (FA) module that uses explicit mechanism chains to support attribution at the run level. Across 4 harnesses, 9 models, and 95 tasks, our evaluation of 3,420 runs yields macro-averaged harness scores ranging from 59.8% to 72.0%, while 31.1% of runs achieve task success despite failing at least one harness criterion. TraceTrial-Bench provides an auditable and reproducible foundation for comparing agent harnesses, diagnosing their failures, and studying how harness design interacts with model behavior.
  </details>

- **[MedCaseAgent: From Multimodal Clinical Evidence to Rare Disease Case Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32258)**  `arXiv:2610.32258`  
  _Hongkang Chu, Yifan Shen, Tianjiao Yu, Zhuoqing Zhong, Jianguo Cao, Meihuan Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated potential in clinical documentation, generating the rigorous, evidence-based reasoning required for unprecedented, atypical, and rare disease case reports remains a significant challenge. We present MedCaseAgent, an end-to-end agentic reasoning paradigm that bridges this gap by synthesizing publication-quality reports from unstructured clinical notes. MedCaseAgent leverages a hierarchical structure, integrating patient-level data mining with iterative, tool-integrated reasoning. By dynamically querying medical literature and knowledge bases, the agent maintains high clinical fidelity and assesses case novelty. The framework is inherently modular, supporting various backbone LLMs in a plug-and-play fashion. To facilitate this research, we release MedCase-150K, a large-scale multimodal clinical case-report corpus, and MedCase-Bench, a robust temporally held-out evaluation suite. We also introduce an open-source model post-trained from Qwen3.5-9B and optimized specifically for this task. Under both LLM-judged and rule-based evaluation, MedCaseAgent outperforms existing general-purpose and specialized medical LLMs and agents. Blinded expert reviews and ablations further support the system's clinical relevance and evidence grounding.
  </details>

- **[RSIHub: A Composable Framework for Agent-Evolution Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32253)**  `arXiv:2610.32253`  
  _Zimu Wang, Xiaobo Wang, Siyu Ye, Jiaqi Li, Silin Chen, Yuling Shi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent-evolution methods use execution feedback to improve prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration, but their implementations often rebuild the same experimental machinery. We present RSIHub, an open-source framework for building, comparing, and extending these methods on shared infrastructure. Recipes compose reusable operators and declare mutable resources; RSIHub supplies execution, evaluation, recovery, and lineage. Researchers can focus on evolution policy while evaluation stays outside the mutable surface and results remain tied to candidates and evaluation conditions. We instantiate AHE, Hyperagents, A-Evolve, and GEPA without modifying the framework core, keeping method-specific logic in recipes and operators. We evaluate them with MiniSWE and Codex on Terminal Bench 2 and $\tau^3$ Banking under fixed model and evaluator configurations. The largest observed overall pass@1 gain is 13.5 percentage points, while method rankings vary across settings and optimization gains do not always transfer to held-out tasks. RSIHub provides a common protocol for comparing evolution policies, inspectable candidate histories, and reusable components for developing new methods.
  </details>

- **[Minimum Description Length Unsupervisedly Separates Law-Grade from Statistical-Grade Knowledge in a Self-Improving Science Agent - and Reveals 86% of Its Own Discoveries Are Statistical](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32245)**  `arXiv:2610.32245`  
  _Wenbin Mao, Pei Zou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems that discover scientific relations now generate candidate "laws" at scale, but the bottleneck has shifted from generation to vetting: which candidates deserve belief? We show that a deployed self-improving science agent - one that has admitted 252 relations through held-out prediction courts - already computes the quantity that answers this question: the Minimum Description Length compression ratio used for model selection doubles, when read under discrete accounting, as an unsupervised epistemic grader. The compression-ranked top band of the agent's ledger recovers exactly the canonical laws among its discoveries (vis-viva across seven planets, Balmer/Rydberg) plus cross-measurement identities - enrichment $p=3.6\times10^{-12}$ under a hypergeometric test - with zero counterexamples and physically coherent band structure. The same lens retroactively reveals that 86% of the ledger compresses nothing ($\mathrm{cr}\approx 1$): the agent had been recording statistical regularities, including a learned predictor with held-out $R^2=0.541$ admitted through the prediction gate, under a narrative of compression as the unified value of knowledge - a narrative that was never true for them. We formalize the resulting dual-axis bookkeeping - prediction value ($R^2$) and compression value (cr) as nearly decoupled axes of knowledge - demonstrate its robustness to quantization, and register forward falsifiable predictions. The episode is, to our knowledge, the first measured instance of a discovery agent auditing its own knowledge ledger by an internal measure and finding itself over-claiming.
  </details>

- **[Spatial Heuristic Strategy based Diffusion Reinforcement Learning for Online 3D Bin Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32241)**  `arXiv:2610.32241`  
  _Tong Li, Qingyang Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional heuristic methods have accumulated mature experience in core objectives, such as space utilization and layout compactness of 3D bin packing, relying on artificially designed rule-based logic. However, they exhibit obvious limitations in generalization ability and adaptability to dynamic scenarios. Diffusion reinforcement learning, which integrates the spatial search capability of reinforcement learning and the data distribution modeling capability of diffusion learning, has achieved certain progress in action distribution modeling and decision compliance constraints. Nevertheless, there remain issues including insufficient perception of the spatial topological structure in 3D bin packing and limited layout optimization capability. To address these problems, we propose a diffusion reinforcement learning method integrated with spatial heuristic strategies, which combines classical bin packing heuristic rules with a diffusion reinforcement learning framework. Specifically, it compresses the action space through heuristic action pre-screening, introduces a graph neural network to model the spatial structure, and designs a composite reward function incorporating flatness to suppress spatial fragmentation, thereby realizing the organic integration of prior knowledge and adaptive optimization. Experimental results demonstrate that the pro
  </details>

- **[Training an MoE LLM in MXFP4: How We Closed the Gap to BF16](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32229)**  `arXiv:2610.32229`  
  _Han Wang, Zhitao Wang, Natalia Frumkin, Xuanwu Yin, Alireza Khodamoradi, Dong Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models (LLMs) in sub-8-bit arithmetic promises substantial gains in memory bandwidth and compute throughput, yet FP4 formats introduce quantization error severe enough to destabilize optimization if left unaddressed. We present ALTO, an open-source training recipe for GPT-OSS-20B, a 20-billion-parameter mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, on the MLPerf Small MoE Training benchmark under the MXFP4 microscaling format. The recipe combines established techniques from Abecassis, Felix et al. [arXiv:2509.25149] (hybrid 1D/2D block quantization, randomized Hadamard transforms, and stochastic rounding) with a simplified weight de-oscillation scheme adapted from TetraJet-v2 [arXiv:2510.27527]. On C4 validation at global batch size 16, MXFP4 + RHT + SR + de-oscillation reaches a validation loss of 3.3350 at 16,128 steps, only 0.007 above the BF16 baseline (3.3283), closing roughly 50% of the gap left by MXFP4 without de-oscillation (3.3418). Measured by steps to reach the MLPerf quality target (validation loss 3.34), de-oscillation cuts the FP4 convergence overhead relative to BF16 from +20.0% to +5.0%. We also report negative end-to-end results from differential gradient estimation, outlier clipping, and macro-block scaling, despite each showing operator-level SNR gains. All experiments use simulated MXFP4 kernels on AMD MI300 hardware and wall-clock speed has not been optimized.
  </details>

- **[Correct Answers, Closed Gates: SolveBind for GUI Agents at the Verification Barrier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32223)**  `arXiv:2610.32223`  
  _Xinhao Song, Haoxuan Peng, Su Su, Hongliang Wu, Sirui Song, Gongshen Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Websites and applications deploy CAPTCHAs and anti-automation mechanisms that use behavioral signals such as interaction trajectories and timing, not outcomes alone, to distinguish humans from automated agents. We call these protected GUI environments, where correctly solving a verification task is insufficient for end-to-end task completion. Agents must turn solver outputs into accepted interactions and adapt execution to runtime feedback under time constraints. We introduce SolveBind, a GUI-agent framework combining hierarchical solving with runtime supervision. A hierarchical solving tree decomposes verification into interdependent Perceive, Ground, Solve, Act, and Verify capabilities. A runtime supervisor grounds action commitments in tool-derived evidence, revises them as evidence arrives, and mediates execution under explicit recovery budgets. Across nine CAPTCHA task families and nine backbone models, SolveBind achieves the highest success rate on eight backbones, outperforming the strongest baseline by 13.3-34.5 percentage points. It achieves the highest success rate on all six AndroidWorld backbones. In a paired GPT-5.5 analysis, MobileAgent produces correct answers but fails dynamic verification on 13 of 90 tasks; SolveBind succeeds on seven of these tasks. With automatic submission, removing binding reduces success from 82.22% to 71.56% and increases latency and token usage. Recovery caps reduce execution cost with a small observed success trade-off: removing them raises success from 82.22% to 83.56%, while increasing latency and token usage to 3.7x and 2.4x their full-supervision values, respectively. These results support evidence-grounded execution control while identifying a success-cost trade-off in recovery and failure boundaries of heuristic submission rules.
  </details>

- **[RxnExBench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Structured Chemical Reaction Extraction from Scientific Literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32221)**  `arXiv:2610.32221`  
  _Jiaxing Sun, Junyuan Gao, Ruikang Zhang, Hao Zheng, Lingli Ge, Xiao An, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Screen Tables and Substrate Scopes are information-dense figures widely used in synthetic chemistry literature, yet automated extraction of structured reaction data from them remains challenging. We introduce RxnExBench, a multimodal benchmark for fine-grained reaction-level evaluation. Built from high-impact chemistry journals, it contains 3,565 expert-annotated reactions and 2,361 molecular structures. The dataset adopts a unified schema with 15 reaction roles and 26 core fields, supporting downstream AI-for-Chemistry tasks such as retrosynthetic planning, yield prediction, and reaction condition recommendation. RxnExBench further provides a reasoning-driven difficulty taxonomy, a modular annotation pipeline, and the Oracle-MolRec protocol, which disentangles molecular recognition from higher-level reasoning. Evaluation of 9 state-of-the-art VLMs shows that performance degrades substantially as schema complexity and reasoning demands increase. Under the Oracle-MolRec setting, results further reveal that accurate molecular recognition alone is insufficient, with multimodal integration and chemistry-aware reasoning remaining the primary bottlenecks. We release all data and code to facilitate future research in chemistry-aware multimodal understanding and AI-for-Chemistry dataset construction. The dataset and code will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PEAR: Adaptive Advantage Reshaping for Perception and Exploration in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32216)**  `arXiv:2610.32216`  
  _Junxin Wang, Yingfeng Luo, Xiangchen Song, Weiqiao Shan, Runsong Zhao, Xinyu Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multimodal reasoning requires a model to reliably utilize visual information while sufficiently exploring diverse reasoning paths. To achieve this goal, prior work combines visual dependency and token entropy to reshape sequence-level advantages, but scaling the contribution of token entropy by visual dependency can leave high-entropy tokens with low visual dependency underweighted, despite their important role in reasoning exploration. To address this limitation, we propose PEAR, an adaptive advantage reshaping method for improving perception and exploration in multimodal reasoning. PEAR retains visual dependency as the base signal and adds an entropy-based exploration gain only at high-entropy positions, with lower visual dependency leading to a larger additional gain. The fused signal is then mapped to positive token-level weights that reshape the sequence-level advantage. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B show that PEAR achieves the highest average performance among evaluated baselines across multimodal reasoning benchmarks, alongside leading results on textual reasoning and general multimodal benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Watermarking Diffusion Language Models with Learned Foresight and Balanced Bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32205)**  `arXiv:2610.32205`  
  _Hyojun Kim, Hyeseon An, Yo-Sub Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Green-red watermarks favor tokens from a green list that a secret key derives from the preceding tokens. Diffusion language models, however, often choose a token before its preceding tokens, and the token later shapes its neighbors' green lists. Prior work therefore tilts each choice to raise the expected number of green tokens at its own and its neighbors' positions. Building on this objective, we first learn two components that prior work keeps fixed: vocabulary bands that serve as green lists, separate competing candidates and keep the null distribution exact, and a foresight predictor that estimates how each candidate shifts its neighbors' predictions. Second, we count green tokens as the detector does, once per distinct context-token pair, so repetition earns nothing. On WaterBench with LLaDA-8B-Instruct, each learned component raises the z-score at equal distortion, and their gains are nearly additive. With a strong watermark, ours detects at least 96% of responses at a 1% false-positive rate while matching the LLM-judge score of unwatermarked text. Ours also detects more responses on Dream and as many or more on code and question answering without a significant loss in task accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-Grounded SLM Behavioral Prediction Architecture for Future Assistance Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32193)**  `arXiv:2610.32193`  
  _Adeyemi Adeseye, Aisvarya Adeseye, Hannu Tenhunen, Jouni Isoaho_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Users often exhibit changes in interaction behavior before explicitly seeking assistance, enabling systems to anticipate future assistance use. This is especially important in high-stakes environments, where delayed assistance can have operational, financial, or safety consequences. However, conventional Large Language Models (LLMs) are often unsuitable for continuous on-device behavioral prediction because of their computational and memory demands. Behavioral patterns are also highly individual and device-specific, motivating local processing that preserves privacy and data sovereignty. Small Language Models (SLMs) offer a promising alternative, but their ability to reason reliably over low-level sequential interaction events remains underexplored. This study introduces Evidence-Grounded Local Behavioral Prediction (ELBP), a four-layer architecture combining deterministic interaction processing, dual-view behavioral representation, SLM-based prediction, and evidence verification with abstention. Three instruction-tuned models of 3B parameters or fewer were evaluated using raw, structured, dual-view, and complete ELBP configurations. The best-performing model, Qwen2.5-3B with ELBP, achieved a full-set F1-score of 0.80 and PR-AUC of 0.84, representing a 15.9\% relative improvement over raw-event prompting and 17.6\% over XGBoost, while full-set ECE decreased by 43.2\% relative to raw-event prompting. With evidence verification and abstention, selective F1 increased to 0.84 at 91\% coverage and evidence support reached 96\%. These results show that structured behavioral representation and evidence-verified selective prediction can improve the reliability of behavioral forecasting in SLMs.
  </details>

- **[$Ψ$-0.6: Adaptive Streams of Thought in a Unified Vision-Language World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32177)**  `arXiv:2610.32177`  
  _Khai Loong Aw, Klemen Kotar, Baihan Zhang, Yifan Liu, Lilian Chen, Atlas Kazemian, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans reason about the physical world by flexibly combining different forms of inference as a problem unfolds. Achieving this flexibility is a major open challenge in AI: capabilities often remain separated across specialized models, limiting how they can be composed and jointly adapted to a task. We introduce $\Psi$-0.6, a unified vision-language world model that adaptively composes perception, language, geometry, and simulation within a shared reasoning process we call streams of thought. These streams interleave images, text, and explicit representations of geometry and motion, all of which the model can generate and interpret through a shared token interface. $\Psi$-0.6 surpasses leading open-weight models zero-shot on multi-view spatial reasoning, with further gains from reasoning through geometric intermediates. It also supports counterfactual simulation: generating and interpreting imagined scenes to determine, for example, whether moving an object would obstruct a pathway. Reinforcement learning from task feedback jointly optimizes the selection, generation, and use of intermediate representations, improving visual question-answering accuracy with gains that transfer to unseen benchmarks. $\Psi$-0.6 offers a path toward adaptive physical reasoning -- not only predicting the physical world but also choosing the representations through which to reason.
  </details>

- **[MPQ: A Message-Passing View of Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32127)**  `arXiv:2610.32127`  
  _Yanshuo Chen, Chenxi Liu, Reza Shirkavand, Heng Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a key technique for efficiently deploying large language models, as it compresses weights into low-bit representations without retraining. Existing PTQ solvers, including GPTQ, LDLQ, and block coordinate descent, commit each weight block to a single codeword before its neighbors are considered, leaving near-optimal alternatives unexplored. In this work, we cast row-wise PTQ as posterior inference over discrete codebooks, in which existing solvers correspond to a zero-temperature, hard-decision limit retaining only the top codeword per block. We propose Message-Passing Quantization (MPQ), which preserves soft posterior information across blocks during inference and commits to hard codewords only at the output stage. MPQ first uses vector approximate message passing to compute approximate blockwise posteriors, then refines a hard-decision initialization by exact block coordinate descent. Across 2-bit lattice and 3- and 4-bit scalar quantization on dense language models from Llama, Qwen, and Mistral families, MPQ improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy over strong hard-decision baselines, and composes favorably with preprocessing techniques such as activation-aware scaling and incoherence processing. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PersonaScienceBench: Evaluating Scientific Agents with Diverse Simulated Researchers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32106)**  `arXiv:2610.32106`  
  _Siyang Wu, Weizhi Zhang, Chen Yang, Esther Xu Fei, Jorin Overwiening, Xu Pan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific agents collaborate with researchers whose expertise and preferences differ, but benchmarks usually hold the collaborator fixed. We introduce PersonaScienceBench: 200 tasks from five benchmarks across four scientific domains. Simulated researchers provide context and feedback according to persona profiles, while task objectives and grading remain fixed. Agents encounter researcher preferences only through conversation. Across eighteen modelconfigurations, task performance, fact recovery, and self-repetition reveal different strengths. Detailed GPT 6 Astra analyses show distinct agent responses across researcher profiles; interaction traces illustrate targeted clar ification and workflow revision. These behaviors can differ even when final scores match. PersonaScienceBench therefore evaluates how agents collaborate with diverse researchers alongside whether they complete the task.
  </details>

- **[Learning Nonlinear Waveform Fusion Across Scales for Integrated Sensing and Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32099)**  `arXiv:2610.32099`  
  _Jiayu Liu, Kai Wan, Shuo Shao, Giuseppe Caire_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fixed source superposition and frequency partitioning constrain how communication content interacts with a probing reference in integrated sensing and communication (ISAC). We learn nonlinear waveform fusion across scales for image transmission and localization of multiple targets in range and angle. Reception uses the information available at each end. Adaptive temporal feature weighting and spectral updates combine image features and a deterministic linearly frequency-modulated reference in a shared complex state, generating a waveform with fixed total energy over the array frame. At the user equipment (UE), the reference and channel state information condition a neural demixer for image reconstruction. At the base station, the realized transmit pulse defines a coherent dictionary for sequential localization with learned local correction, physical refinement, and residual updates. We compare the proposed waveforms with superposition in the time domain and learned spectral linear fusion on 512 independent test images, using a shared fixed UE receiver and a baseline sensing receiver using coherent peak search. Averaged across eleven task weights, the proposed waveforms reduce normalized localization error by 29.0\% and 27.0\%, respectively, while increasing mean peak signal-to-noise ratio by 1.40 and 0.82~dB. With the proposed waveforms fixed, learned local correction reduces mean normalized localization error by 12.2\% relative to the same sequential receiver without learned correction.
  </details>

- **[DELA: Learning Controllable Edits for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32096)**  `arXiv:2610.32096`  
  _Xinsong Feng, Chenan Wang, Leshu Tang, Johnny Liu, Dong Li, Da Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) support bidirectional denoising and iterative refinement, but generation remains largely controlled by token-level masking schedules. This leaves draft-level refinement implicit and limits explicit control over when to insert missing content, delete redundant spans, revisit uncertain regions, or stop editing. We introduce Diffusion Editing Language Adapter (DELA), a plug-in framework that enables controllable draft refinement with a frozen DLM. DELA operates on clean, variable-length drafts using four explicit editing actions: Insert, Delete, Remask, and Stop. The controller determines when and where to edit the draft, while the frozen DLM fills masked regions through denoising, separating high-level edit control from token-level generation. A naive single-phase editor would be computationally expensive because arbitrary interior edits can disrupt prefix stability and limit reliable KV-cache reuse. To address this, DELA uses a two-phase decoding procedure: a fast, append-only drafting phase that supports KV-cache reuse, followed by a global refinement phase that enables targeted non-monotonic edits to the full draft. We train the controller using a staged curriculum that begins with learning local edit priors from synthetic recovery tasks and mined actions that improve draft quality. Subsequent stages refine edit selection through rollout-based outcome preferences and alignment with downstream task feedback. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation show that DELA achieves gains of up to 5.5 percentage points over direct diffusion decoding while keeping the backbone frozen. DELA thus provides a modular approach to explicit draft-level control in diffusion language models.
  </details>

- **[DriveMind: Mind-Evolving Belief Tracking for Closed-Loop Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32069)**  `arXiv:2610.32069`  
  _Yan Shi, Kehua Chen, Jiarui Qi, Yihong Tang, Haichen Liao, Yinhai Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can perceive a traffic scene, reason about it, and propose a driving action within a single model. However, existing MLLM-based drivers reason from scratch at each frame and commit to a single explanation, discarding earlier hypotheses about the scene. When the scene is ambiguous, the agent locks in on the wrong interpretation and acts on it before evidence accumulates. In this paper, we propose DriveMind, an MLLM-based driving framework that keeps several hypotheses about the scene alive at the same time and updates their weights as new frames arrive.
