We introduce SteerVTE, a unified framework that steers a frozen video diffusion model to perform precise Video Text Editing through style and glyph control. Built on a frozen diffusion transformer, SteerVTE attaches a lightweight text context adapter with two complementary modules: a style encoder capturing the original text's visual attributes, and dual-granularity glyph encoders encoding the target text at both the line and character levels. To overcome the inherently weak text rendering priors of video foundation models, we further propose a glyph-aware spatial-focal loss and a three-stage progressive training curriculum that scales from image to video data. To support large-scale training, we also develop an automatic synthesis pipeline and construct SteerVTE-1M, a dataset of one million triplets spanning diverse scenes, fonts, and stylistic effects. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SteerVTE substantially outperforms existing video editing baselines across text accuracy, style consistency, and temporal coherence. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OmniSpace: Efficient Geometry Awareness for Autonomous Vehicles MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22617)**  `arXiv:2606.22617`  
  _Hao Vo, Phu Loc Nguyen, Khoa Vo, Sieu Tran, Duc Minh Nguyen, Ngo Xuan Cuong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on 2D visual tasks, yet enhancing their spatial intelligence for real-world applications such as Autonomous Vehicles (AV) remains an open challenge. Existing geometry-aware MLLMs typically rely on auxiliary 3D models at inference time, introducing pipeline complexity and the risk of cascading failures. In this paper, we present OmniSpace, a simple yet effective plug-and-play paradigm for geometry-aware spatial reasoning from purely 2D observations. Motivated by our finding that current MLLMs are bottlenecked by weak cross-view correspondence and depth estimation, OmniSpace introduces a Camera Pose Injector, a Multi-view Epipolar Attention module, and a 3D Geometric Distillation objective that jointly address these two limitations by transferring geometric knowledge into the model. Extensive experiments show that OmniSpace surpasses existing methods on planning benchmarks (nuScenes, Bench2Drive), risk detection (nuInstruct), language (Omnidrive), and generalization (DriveBench).
  </details>

- **[From Frames to Temporal Graphs: In-Context Egocentric Action Recognition with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15417)**  `arXiv:2606.15417`  
  _Bessie Dominguez-Dager, Francisco Gomez-Donoso, Miguel Cazorla, Marc Pollefeys, Daniel Barath, Zuria Bauer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action reasoning in egocentric video requires capturing fine-grained transitions of hand-object interactions, a task where general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) often struggle when operating directly on raw pixels. We propose to decouple visual perception from symbolic reasoning by converting videos into Temporal Action Graphs. In a multi-stage prompting pipeline, we first generate dense natural language narratives over short temporal windows as a semantic bottleneck, then formalize them into structured, open-vocabulary graph representations. On the EGTEA and Epic-Kitchens-100 datasets, the symbolic representation unlocks efficient in-context learning: few-shot graph demonstrations yield substantial accuracy gains over zero-shot frame-based inference. Even in the zero-shot setting, graph-based reasoning remains competitive with pixel-based inference despite potential pretraining contamination favoring the latter. Across 11 open-weight VLMs from 6 model families ranging from 2B to 235B parameters, our findings indicate that current VLMs are more effective as symbolic reasoners than as direct visual observers. By projecting video into the language domain, we provide a scalable, fine-tuning-free alternative to end-to-end approaches that better leverages these models' latent reasoning strengths. The code will be made public.
  </details>

- **[DisPOSE: Projected Polystochastic Diffusion for Self-Supervised Multi-View 3D Human Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07419)**  `arXiv:2606.07419`  
  _Tony Danjun Wang, Tolga Birdal, Nassir Navab, Lennart Bastian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering 3D human poses for multiple individuals from different camera views is a fundamental bottleneck for analyzing interacting behaviors. Existing self-supervised approaches leverage synthetic catalogues of 3D poses; however, this leads to poor generalization in real-world scenarios due to distribution shifts. We therefore introduce DisPOSE, a self-supervised framework that approximates the inherently discrete multi-view person-assignment problem as a generative diffusion process over the space of polystochastic tensors. By employing differentiable Sinkhorn projections during denoising, our model learns to guide solutions toward valid and feasible assignments based on 2D image priors. The complete 3D skeletons of localized individuals are then regressed using a Hypergraph-Convolutional Decoder that explicitly models relational structures and articulated joints across multiple views. The proposed approach outperforms current state-of-the-art self-supervised methods on standard datasets and demonstrates strong performance on a newly proposed benchmark featuring highly occluded scenes from surgical operating rooms. Our diffusion-based localization demonstrates high label efficiency, retaining 99% of its performance with only 10% of the pseudo-labels. Notably, disentangling the assignment and root regression components while maintaining differentiability makes DisPOSE nearly agnostic to different camera arrangements. Project site and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[StemBind: When MLLMs Get Lost Between Rules and Instances in Abstract Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00148)**  `arXiv:2606.00148`  
  _Xixiang He, Baiqi Wu, Xingming Li, Ao Cheng, Qiyao Sun, Xuanyu Ji, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often know the rule but pick the wrong answer: on abstract visual reasoning (AVR) tasks, a model can describe what it sees and name the underlying pattern, yet still fail to choose the matching candidate. Existing AVR benchmarks cannot detect this because they collapse perception, rule induction, and answer selection into a single right-or-wrong signal. We introduce StemBind, a shared-stem diagnostic benchmark that probes the same visual stem with three aligned questions: Perception (what is in the image), Rule (what pattern governs it), and Full (which option completes it), so a final-answer error can be attributed to a specific sub-step on the same evidence. StemBind contains 2,298 curated knowledge-light stems across nine auditable visual operations, totaling 19,533 P/R/F tasks, with each full item annotated by Sternberg's four reasoning stages (S1 Encode, S2 Infer, S3 Map, S4 Apply). Evaluating 24 frontier MLLM configurations yields four findings. (i) The R-F chasm: rule accuracy exceeds full-item accuracy on 22 of 24 models, so most failures happen after the rule is identified. (ii) A persistent binding gap: even when P and R are both correct on the same stem, models still answer F incorrectly 51.2% of the time. (iii) The bottleneck is S3: process diagnostics and Stage-wise Stimulus Augmentation localize the dominant failure to rule-to-instance mapping. (iv) Scaling and thinking do not help: neither larger models nor explicit thinking mode reliably closes the gap, and thinking even lowers rule and full-item accuracy. StemBind reframes AVR evaluation from final-answer ranking to locating where abstract visual reasoning breaks down, identifying rule-to-instance binding as a concrete next target for vision-grounded reasoning.
  </details>

- **[SetCon: Towards Open-Ended Referring Segmentation via Set-Level Concept Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20110)**  `arXiv:2605.20110`  
  _Zhixiong Zhang, Yizhuo Li, Shuangrui Ding, Yuhang Zang, Shengyuan Ding, Long Xing, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring segmentation grounds natural-language queries to pixel-level masks, but extending it to complex scenarios with multiple instances, cross-category groups, or open-ended target sets remains challenging. Previous Large Vision Language Model (LVLM)-based methods represent referred targets with one or more special tokens sequentially, treating multiple targets as separate outputs rather than a coherent set and offering little incentive to capture set-level properties such as completeness and mutual exclusivity. We reformulate open-ended referring segmentation as explicit set-level concept prediction and propose Set-Concept Segmentation (SetCon), which uses LVLM-generated natural-language concepts, instead of segmentation-specific tokens, as semantic conditions for joint mask-set decoding. A hierarchical semantic decomposition first predicts a shared set-level concept defining the target scope and then refines it into fine-grained concept groups aligned with target subsets. To support this, a two-stage annotation pipeline augments existing reasoning segmentation datasets with hierarchical semantic supervision (236k samples, 784k concept phrases). SetCon achieves state-of-the-art results on image benchmarks (+3.3 gIoU on gRefCOCO, +12.1 gIoU on MUSE), with margins that grow as the number of referred targets increases. The concept interface also transfers to video under a detect-and-track setting, yielding new state-of-the-art results on seven referring video benchmarks, including +10.9 J&F on MeViS and +12.4 J&F on Ref-SeCVOS.
  </details>

- **[VLMs Trace Without Tracking: Diagnosing Failures in Visual Path Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15672)**  `arXiv:2605.15672`  
  _Hyesoo Hong, Min Soo Kim, Wonje Jeung, Sangyeon Yoon, Dongjae Jeon, Albert No_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) perform strongly on multimodal benchmarks, yet their ability to reliably follow continuous visual structure remains unclear. We focus on path following, a basic operation that requires following a selected path through successive visual continuations. Before considering complex factors such as crossings or overlaps, we first test whether current VLMs can robustly follow a simple continuous line under controlled settings that remove such confounds. Across frontier models, we find that tracing is already fragile, with this vulnerability manifesting as unstable path preservation in the presence of other nearby paths. Analysis of internal representations shows that information needed to determine the correct path continuation becomes weaker, with this degradation already emerging in the visual encoding stage. Common remedies do not resolve this weakness: model scaling provides limited improvement, additional reasoning provides uneven gains at substantial cost, and explicit tracing instructions fail to stabilize performance. The same path-preservation failures and sensitivity to nearby paths persist in visually richer and more realistic settings, suggesting that current VLMs lack a robust mechanism for following a selected path.
  </details>

- **[IGLOSS: Image Generation for Lidar Open-vocabulary Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01361)**  `arXiv:2604.01361`  
  _Nermin Samet, Gilles Puy, Renaud Marlet_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a new method for the zero-shot open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) of 3D automotive lidar data. To circumvent the recognized image-text modality gap that is intrinsic to approaches based on Vision Language Models (VLMs) such as CLIP, our method relies instead on image generation from text, to create prototype images. Given a 3D network distilled from a 2D Vision Foundation Model (VFM), we then label a point cloud by matching 3D point features with 2D image features of these prototypes. Our method is state-of-the-art for OVSS on nuScenes and SemanticKITTI. Code, pre-trained models and generated images are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Half-Truths Break Similarity-Based Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23906)**  `arXiv:2602.23906`  
  _Bora Kargi, Arnas Uselis, Seong Joon Oh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a text description is extended with an additional detail, image-text similarity should drop if that detail is wrong. We show that CLIP-style dual encoders often violate this intuition: appending a plausible but incorrect object or relation to an otherwise correct description can increase the similarity score. We call such cases half-truths. On COCO, CLIP prefers the correct shorter description only 34.0% of the time, and performance drops to 27.8% when the added detail is a relation. We trace this vulnerability to weak supervision on caption parts: contrastive training aligns full sentences but does not explicitly enforce that individual entities and relations are grounded. We propose CS-CLIP (Component-Supervised CLIP), which decomposes captions into entity and relation units, constructs a minimally edited foil for each unit, and fine-tunes the model to score the correct unit above its foil while preserving standard dual-encoder inference. CS-CLIP raises half-truth accuracy to 76.4% and improves average performance on established compositional benchmarks by 5.8 points, suggesting that reducing half-truth errors aligns with broader gains in compositional understanding.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Through the Chain: Understanding and Mitigating Hallucinations in Multimodal Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03380)**  `arXiv:2602.03380`  
  _Hao Fang, Jinyu Li, Jiawei Kong, Tianqu Zhuang, Kuofeng Gao, Bin Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal reasoning models (MLRMs) have exhibited impressive capabilities, they remain prone to hallucinations, and effective solutions are still underexplored. In this paper, we experimentally analyze the hallucination cause and propose C3PO, a training-based mitigation framework comprising \textbf{C}hain-of-Thought \textbf{C}ompression and \textbf{C}ontrastive \textbf{P}reference \textbf{O}ptimization. Firstly, we identify that introducing reasoning mechanisms exacerbates models' reliance on language priors while overlooking visual inputs, which can produce CoTs with reduced visual cues but redundant text tokens. To this end, we propose to selectively filter redundant thinking tokens for a more compact and signal-efficient CoT representation that preserves task-relevant information while suppressing noise. In addition, we observe that the quality of the reasoning trace largely determines whether hallucination emerges in subsequent responses. To leverage this insight, we introduce a reasoning-enhanced preference tuning scheme that constructs training pairs using high-quality AI feedback. We further design a multimodal hallucination-inducing mechanism that elicits models' inherent hallucination patterns via carefully crafted inducers, yielding informative negative signals for contrastive correction. We provide theoretical justification for the effectiveness and demonstrate consistent hallucination reduction across diverse MLRMs and benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SpatialMem: Metric-Aligned Long-Horizon Video Memory for Language Grounding and QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14895)**  `arXiv:2601.14895`  
  _Xinyi Zheng, Yunze Liu, Chi-Hao Wu, Fan Zhang, Hao Zheng, Wenqi Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  SpatialMem is a memory-based system which integrates 3D geometry, semantics, and language with one another in one combined, queryable representation. Starting with casually recorded egocentric RGB video, SpatialMem recreates metrically scaled interior scenes, recognizes structural 3D anchors (walls, doors, windows) as the first-layer scaffold, and associates open-vocabulary object nodes with evidence patches, visual embeddings, and two-layer descriptions to 3D coordinates, enabling spatial-relation reasoning, language-directed retrieval, and QA without special sensors. We test at scale on public benchmarks. On VSI-Bench ($288$ videos, $5{,}130$ questions) SpatialMem scores $37.5$ overall with RGB alone, and its margin is precisely where a metric memory should earn it (room size $41.4$ vs.\ $24.4$ with a same-size frame model). On the episodic-memory task of OpenEQA ($953$ questions) the memory serialised to text is lower than the blind control, but as evidence-frame retrieval it recovers frame-level accuracy, matching uniform sampling at an equal eight-frame budget and pulling ahead as the budget tightens, with every answer grounded in linked 3D evidence. On $12$ ScanNet scenes evaluated using instance-level official annotations, the anchor-first hierarchy assigns the retrieved object to the proper structural parent where open-vocabulary 3D scene graphs essentially cannot ($0.24$ compared to $0.01$ with matched input), a capability difference instead of an overall win. The queryable index has a cost of $14$\,KB per scene without the linked evidence frames, and we make available the suites, memories and code.
  </details>

- **[Attention-space Contrastive Guidance for Efficient Hallucination Mitigation in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13707)**  `arXiv:2601.13707`  
  _Yujin Jo, Sangyoon Bae, Taesup Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucinations in large vision--language models (LVLMs) often arise when language priors dominate over visual evidence, leading to object misidentification and visually inconsistent descriptions. We address this problem by framing hallucination mitigation as contrastive guidance that steers generation toward visually grounded and semantically faithful text. We propose Attention-space Contrastive Guidance (ACG), a training-free, single-pass method that operates directly in self-attention layers, where hallucination-inducing cross-modal biases emerge. ACG constructs both image-conditioned and approximate text-only attention paths within a single forward pass, enabling efficient guidance before errors accumulate at the output layer. Because this masking-based surrogate can introduce approximation bias, we further apply a lightweight orthogonal projection that suppresses components aligned with the text-only path, yielding a more visually grounded correction. Experiments on CHAIR and POPE show that ACG improves faithfulness over existing training-free baselines while maintaining caption quality, reducing latency by up to $2\times$ compared to multi-pass contrastive decoding methods.
  </details>

- **[Demo: Generative AI helps Radiotherapy Planning with User Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08996)**  `arXiv:2512.08996`  
  _Riqiang Gao, Simon Arberet, Martin Kraus, Han Liu, Wilko F.A.R. Verbakel, Dorin Comaniciu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiotherapy planning is a highly complex process that often varies significantly across institutions and individual planners. Most existing deep learning approaches for 3D dose prediction rely on reference plans as ground truth during training, which can inadvertently bias models toward specific planning styles or institutional preferences. In this study, we introduce a novel generative model that predicts 3D dose distributions based solely on user-defined preference flavors. These customizable preferences enable planners to prioritize specific trade-offs between organs-at-risk (OARs) and planning target volumes (PTVs), offering greater flexibility and personalization. Designed for seamless integration with clinical treatment planning systems, our approach assists users in generating high-quality plans efficiently. Comparative evaluations demonstrate that our method can surpasses the Varian RapidPlan model in both adaptability and plan quality in some scenarios.
  </details>

- **[BrainRotViT: Transformer-ResNet Hybrid for Explainable Modeling of Brain Aging from 3D sMRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15188)**  `arXiv:2511.15188`  
  _Wasif Jalal, Md Nafiu Rahman, Atif Hasan Rahman, M.Sohel Rahman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate brain-age estimation from structural MRI provides a valuable biomarker for studying healthy aging and neurodegenerative disease. We propose BrainRotViT (Brain ResNet over trained Vision Transformer), a two-stage, slice-based (2.5D) framework that approximates volumetric context while combining the strengths of CNNs and transformers. A Vision Transformer is pretrained on an auxiliary age-sex composite classification task and subsequently frozen. Its per-slice embeddings are stacked into a 160 x 768 feature matrix and processed as a pseudo-image by a lightweight 2D residual CNN regressor, with subject sex incorporated at the final prediction layer. Trained and validated on 5,108 samples from 11 multi-site datasets and tested on four independent held-out cohorts comprising 948 samples, BrainRotViT achieves a validation mean absolute error (MAE) of 3.43 years (Pearson r = 0.98, $R^2$ = 0.96, Spearman $\rho$ = 0.97) and cross-cohort MAEs of 4.79-5.35 years, outperforming retrained 3D-ResNet, SFCN, 3D-ViT, Global-Local Transformer, Triamese-ViT, and TSAN baselines. After age-bias correction and covariate adjustment, brain-age gap is significantly associated with Alzheimer's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and autism spectrum disorder. An interpretability pipeline combining ViT attention with guided backpropagation, saliency sanity checks, and AAL3-based anatomical localization identifies aging-related regions including the occipital lobe, cerebellum, precentral and postcentral gyri, temporal lobes, and medial superior frontal gyrus. BrainRotViT thus provides an efficient and interpretable bridge between CNN and transformer paradigms, demonstrating cross-cohort generalization while yielding biologically meaningful insights into brain aging.
  </details>

- **[ProCLIP: Progressive Vision-Language Alignment via LLM-based Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18795)**  `arXiv:2510.18795`  
  _Xiaoxing Hu, Kaicheng Yang, Ziqi Ye, Ziyang Gong, Qi Ming, Zonghao Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The original CLIP text encoder is limited by a maximum input length of 77 tokens, which hampers its ability to effectively process long texts and perform fine-grained semantic understanding. In addition, the CLIP text encoder lacks support for multilingual inputs. All these limitations significantly restrict its applicability across a broader range of tasks. Recent studies have attempted to replace the CLIP text encoder with an LLM-based embedder to enhance its ability in processing long texts, multilingual understanding, and fine-grained semantic comprehension. However, because the representation spaces of LLMs and the vision-language space of CLIP are pretrained independently without alignment priors, direct alignment using contrastive learning can disrupt the intrinsic vision-language alignment in the CLIP image encoder, leading to an underutilization of the knowledge acquired during pre-training. To address this challenge, we propose ProCLIP, a curriculum learning-based progressive vision-language alignment framework to effectively align the CLIP image encoder with an LLM-based embedder. Specifically, ProCLIP first distills knowledge from CLIP's text encoder into the LLM-based embedder to leverage CLIP's rich pretrained knowledge while establishing initial alignment between the LLM embedder and CLIP image encoder. Subsequently, ProCLIP further aligns the CLIP image encoder with the LLM-based embedder through image-text contrastive tuning, employing self-distillation regularization to avoid overfitting. To achieve a more effective alignment, instance semantic alignment loss and embedding structure alignment loss are employed during representation inheritance and contrastive tuning. The Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Closer Look at Evaluation of Text-to-Video Models Under Camera Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07441)**  `arXiv:2510.07441`  
  _Nithin C. Babu, Aniruddha Mahapatra, Harsh Rangwani, Rajiv Soundararajan, Kuldeep Kulkarni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing text-to-video (T2V) evaluation benchmarks, such as VBench and EvalCrafter, predominantly contain either subject-centric prompts or static camera scenes, overlooking the importance of camera motion, essential for producing cinematic shots with realistic scenes. Due to this, the metrics developed in these works often produce incorrect results in the presence of camera motion. To fill this gap, we introduce DynamicEval, a benchmark comprising of systematically curated prompts that emphasize dynamic camera motion, paired with 45k human annotations on video pairs. DynamicEval evaluates two key dimensions of video quality: the consistency of the foreground subjects and the consistency of the background scene. To address the shortcomings of existing metrics under camera motion, we introduce two novel metrics: 1) Tracker-FG, which evaluates subject consistency by assessing the keypoint tracks of the subject. 2) Debiased Motion Smoothness, for background consistency, devoid from issues of occlusions/disocclusions arising from camera and foreground subject movements. Extensive experiments on the large-scale DynamicEval benchmark demonstrate that our proposed metrics achieve stronger correlations with human preferences at both the video level, and the model level with an increase of 0.32 in average Pearson Linear Correlation.
  </details>

- **[NS-Net: Decoupling CLIP Semantic Information through NULL-Space for Generalizable AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01248)**  `arXiv:2508.01248`  
  _Jiazhen Yan, Fan Wang, Ziwen He, Ziqiang Li, Zhangjie Fu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid progress of generative models, such as GANs and diffusion models, has facilitated the creation of highly realistic images, raising growing concerns over their misuse in security-sensitive domains. While existing detectors perform well under known generative settings, they often fail to generalize to unknown generative models, especially when semantic content between real and fake images is closely aligned. In this paper, we revisit the use of CLIP features for AI-generated image detection and uncover a critical limitation: the high-level semantic information embedded in CLIP's visual features hinders effective discrimination. To address this, we propose the NS-Net, a novel detection framework that leverages NULL-Space projection to decouple semantic information from CLIP's visual features, followed by contrastive learning to capture intrinsic distributional differences between real and generated images. Furthermore, we design a Patch Selection strategy to preserve fine-grained artifacts by selecting patches with the highest and lowest spectral entropy. Extensive experiments on an open-world benchmark comprising images generated by 47 diverse generative models show that NS-Net outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods, achieving a 5.0\% improvement in detection accuracy, thereby demonstrating strong generalization across both GAN- and diffusion-based image generation techniques. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Camera Movement Estimation and Path Correction using the Combination of Modified A-SIFT and Stereo System for 3D Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.17668)**  `arXiv:2503.17668`  
  _Usha Kumari, Shuvendu Rana_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Creating accurate and efficient 3D models poses significant challenges, particularly in addressing large viewpoint variations, computational complexity, and alignment discrepancies. Efficient camera path generation can help resolve these issues. In this context, a modified version of the Affine Scale-Invariant Feature Transform (ASIFT) is proposed to extract more matching points with reduced computational overhead, ensuring an adequate number of inliers for precise camera rotation angle estimation. Additionally, a novel two-camera-based rotation correction model is introduced to mitigate small rotational errors, further enhancing accuracy. Furthermore, a stereo camera-based translation estimation and correction model is implemented to determine camera movement in 3D space by altering the Structure From Motion (SFM) model. Finally, the novel combination of ASIFT and two camera-based SFM models provides an accurate camera movement trajectory in 3D space. Experimental results show that the proposed camera movement approach achieves 99.9% accuracy compared to the actual camera movement path and outperforms state-of-the-art camera path estimation methods. By leveraging this accurate camera path, the system facilitates the creation of precise 3D models, making it a robust solution for applications requiring high fidelity and efficiency in 3D reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[MouseGPT: A Large-scale Vision-Language Model for Mouse Behavior Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10212)**  `arXiv:2503.10212`  
  _Teng Xu, Taotao Zhou, Youjia Wang, Peng Yang, Simin Tang, Kuixiang Shao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analyzing animal behavior is crucial in neuroscience, yet deciphering its intricate dynamics remains challenging. Traditional machine vision approaches detect spontaneous behaviors but are constrained by limited interpretability and reliance on manual labeling, restricting exploration of the full behavioral spectrum. Here, we introduce MouseGPT, an integrated vision-language model and analytical framework that translates raw video of animal activity into natural language to automate open-vocabulary behavior analysis. Built upon a comprehensive dataset of over 42 million frames spanning diverse psychiatric conditions, MouseGPT maps multi-view or single-camera recordings directly into computable semantic representations. An agentic framework leverages these representations for detailed behavior profiling, cross-view stable clustering, and zero-shot novel behavior discovery without requiring labor-intensive annotation. Surpassing conventional kinematics-based pipelines and generalist vision-language models in behavioral precision and adaptability, MouseGPT establishes continuous semantic representations as a powerful tool for deciphering complex ethological dynamics in animal models.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Vision Transformer with Prototypes for Interpretable Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08997)**  `arXiv:2502.08997`  
  _Luisa Gallée, Meinrad Beer, Michael Götz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explainability is an important requirement for machine learning models in high-risk domains such as medicine. We introduce HierViT, a hierarchical Vision Transformer that combines competitive predictive performance with multiple mechanisms to support the interpretation of its predictions. The model first predicts clinically relevant visual appearance attributes, which are then used to infer the final target prediction through a hierarchical reasoning process. To facilitate understanding of the intermediate representations, attribute-specific prototypes are visualized as representative image examples, while attribute-specific attention maps highlight image regions associated with each visual feature. Together, these attribute-level components provide complementary views of the model's reasoning by linking representative image examples and spatial attention to semantically defined visual features. We evaluate HierViT on two medical benchmark datasets: LIDC-IDRI for lung nodule assessment and derm7pt for skin lesion classification. Across both datasets, HierViT achieves competitive predictive performance while providing attribute-level prototypes and attention visualizations that support the interpretation of its predictions.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Table Recognition with Vision LLMs: A Benchmark and Neighbor-Guided Toolchain Reasoner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.20662)**  `arXiv:2412.20662`  
  _Yitong Zhou, Mingyue Cheng, Qingyang Mao, Jiahao Wang, Feiyang Xu, Xin Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained foundation models have recently made significant progress in table-related tasks such as table understanding and reasoning. However, recognizing the structure and content of unstructured tables using Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) remains under-explored. To bridge this gap, we propose a benchmark based on a hierarchical design philosophy to evaluate the recognition capabilities of VLLMs in training-free scenarios. Through in-depth evaluations, we find that low-quality image input is a significant bottleneck in the recognition process. Drawing inspiration from this, we propose the Neighbor-Guided Toolchain Reasoner (NGTR) framework, which is characterized by integrating diverse lightweight tools for visual operations aimed at mitigating issues with low-quality images. Specifically, we transfer a tool selection experience from a similar neighbor to the input and design a reflection module to supervise the tool invocation process. Extensive experiments on public datasets demonstrate that our approach significantly enhances the recognition capabilities of the vanilla VLLMs. We believe that the benchmark and framework could provide an alternative solution to table recognition.
  </details>

- **[STATIC: Surface-normal-guided Two-branch Alignment and Temporal Integration for Consistent Video Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.01090)**  `arXiv:2412.01090`  
  _Sunghun Yang, Minhyeok Lee, Suhwan Cho, Jungho Lee, Sangyoun Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video depth estimation is essential for applications such as autonomous driving, AR/VR, and robotics. Recent transformer-based single-image monocular depth estimation models perform well on a single image but struggle with depth consistency across video frames. Traditional methods aim to improve temporal consistency using multi-frame temporal modules or explicit priors like optical flow and camera parameters. However, these approaches face issues such as high memory use, reduced performance with dynamic or irregular motion, and a heavy reliance on the accuracy of external motion estimators. We propose STATIC, a novel model that independently learns temporal consistency in geometrically stable and unstable regions using only two adjacent frames. By leveraging intrinsic geometric cues rather than external motion priors, STATIC generates a depth-derived geometry-aware difference mask from surface normals to identify regions with inter-frame geometric variation by measuring directional variance. For geometrically stable regions, the Masked Stable (MS) module enhances temporal consistency by focusing on structurally stable regions. For geometrically unstable regions, the Surface Normal Similarity (SNS) module models inter-frame correspondences and ensures temporal consistency by measuring feature similarities between frames. A final refinement integrates the independently learned stable and unstable features, enabling STATIC to improve consistent depth prediction across consecutive frames. Experiments on KITTI, NYUv2, and Sintel demonstrate strong depth accuracy and temporal consistency, as well as stable performance across the tested frame-rate and camera-motion conditions.
  </details>

- **[Where Rounding Happens Matters: Rounding-Location Alignment Stabilizes LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32951)**  `arXiv:2610.32951`  
  _Yijing Ke, Tianle Cai, Ruosong Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for large language models often uses separate systems for inference and training. Even with identical weights and BF16 tensors, implementation differences can make an intended on-policy update off-policy and destabilize training. Existing remedies align the entire inference and training stacks, change global precision, or modify the learning algorithm. We ask whether aligning selected parts of the execution stack can stabilize BF16 RL while other implementation differences remain. We align training-side rounding locations with inference in three regions---RoPE, SwiGLU, and residual RMSNorm---by retaining selected intermediates in FP32 until their BF16 outputs. Across four Qwen-family models, aligning all three regions prevents the collapse observed under the default BF16 baseline; RoPE-path alignment alone also suffices in the studied runs, despite remaining numerical discrepancies. The interventions keep the inference engine, matrix-multiplication and attention kernels, and learning objective unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Recommender regret for improving regularised RL estimation with bandits and MCTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32910)**  `arXiv:2610.32910`  
  _Danilo Vucetic, Alexandre Larouche, Mohammad Pedramfar, Audrey Durand, Gauthier Gidel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Regularised reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms rely on estimators, like Monte Carlo tree search or one-step look-ahead, to provide policy, value, or Q-value estimates in training and inference. However, existing bandit-based estimation frameworks evaluate sampling efficiency, not the quality of estimates. We address this by formalising estimate generation as a recommendation process, treating bandit-based sampling as an improvement operator. To evaluate the quality of a recommendation (estimate), we define regulariser-aware recommender regrets, analysing their lower bounds, and proving that simple algorithms like uniform sampling can achieve these bounds. Finally, we unify bandit- and search-based target improvement under recommender MCTS. Across synthetic trees, arcade games, and scientific discovery tasks, recommenders often yield faster target convergence and superior downstream performance. Our experiments characterise for the first time the potential of regularised MCTS in scientific applications.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Step Transition Predictions: A Bellman Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32887)**  `arXiv:2610.32887`  
  _Chenbei Lu, Zaiwei Chen, Tongxin Li, Chenye Wu, Adam Wierman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step predictions of future transition realizations can improve reinforcement learning, but their value remains difficult to quantify. Directly including these predictions in the state can lead to exponential state-space growth, making direct dynamic programming computationally expensive. We study discounted finite MDPs under a block-feedback protocol: at the start of each K-period block, the decision maker observes $K$-step probabilistic transition predictions for a subset of actions and adapts decisions to observed states within the block. We show that the optimal value satisfies a Bayesian Bellman equation on the original state space. This representation reveals that predictive information creates value by recursively changing the local order of maximization and expectation in the Bellman expansion. We formalize this effect through a Bellman--Jensen analysis and quantify the effects of prediction horizon, error, action coverage, and within-block feedback. We propose block-feedback Bayesian offline learning with online adaptation (BF--BOLA), a model-based algorithm for learning near-optimal policies, and establish upper and lower sample-complexity bounds that match up to logarithmic factors in the stated regimes. The results show that predictions can reduce transition learning to uncovered rows and that learning a near-optimal policy can require fewer prediction samples than estimating the Bayesian value function.
  </details>

- **[PoKER: A Probability of Kill Estimation Rate Model for Air-to-Air Missiles Using Machine Learning on Stochastic Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32864)**  `arXiv:2610.32864`  
  _Joao P. A. Dantas, Andre N. Costa, Diego Geraldo, Marcos R. O. A. Maximo, Takashi Yoneyama_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work introduces PoKER, a novel probabilistic model engineered to optimize missile launch effectiveness in air-to-air scenarios, specifically within Beyond Visual Range (BVR) air combat. Unlike conventional Weapon Engagement Zone (WEZ) models that delineate zones based on static distances such as maximum, minimum, and no-escape ranges, PoKER applies machine learning to predict kill probabilities more accurately by integrating the stochastic behaviors of targets and missile miss distances. This model dynamically evaluates target behavior, greatly expanding the predictive capabilities of engagement analysis. By factoring in elements such as warhead lethality, target and shooter orientations, and the specific conditions of engagement, PoKER provides important insights into engagement dynamics and quantifies success probabilities. Consequently, it can potentially be an important tool for BVR air combat pilots, improving operational decision-making within this specialized combat domain.
  </details>

- **[Sustainability of TSFM Deployment and Pretraining: From Carbon Footprint Analysis to Mitigation via Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32859)**  `arXiv:2610.32859`  
  _Cécile Luc, Nathanaël Lemonnier, Ievgen Redko_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) offer strong zero-shot forecasting capabilities, but their high pretraining cost raises a practical sustainability question: does this upfront investment pay off over a full deployment lifecycle, compared to supervised standalone models that must be periodically retrained? We model the cumulative carbon cost of different approaches across a realistic industrial scenario: Chronos-2 amortizes its pretraining cost within months for large or frequently queried deployments, but a classical model remains cheaper indefinitely for small or rarely queried ones. For cases where reducing the TSFM's own cost matters, we show that removing a single encoder block (7.9% of parameters) via a gradient-free pruning criterion yields a consistent 6 to 9% reduction in FLOPs, CO2 emissions, and latency, at essentially no cost to zero-shot forecasting performance across a 25-task benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Near-Optimal List Replicability from Littlestone Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32853)**  `arXiv:2610.32853`  
  _Ari Blondal, Pooya Hatami, Chavdar Lalov, Sivan Tretiak_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the list replicability number in the realizable PAC learning framework and prove that every concept class $H$ over a countable domain with Littlestone dimension $d$ and dual VC dimension $v^*$ satisfies \[ \mathrm{LR}(H)\leq 2^{O(d\min\{\log d,v^*\})}. \] This matches the worst-case lower bound of $2^{\Omega(d)}$ up to a logarithmic factor in the exponent and exponentially improves the previous best upper bound $\mathrm{LR}(H)\le 2^{2^{O(d)}}$ of Bun, Livni, and Moran [FOCS 2020]. Unlike earlier bounds of $2^{O_\epsilon(d^2)}$ for fixed accuracy $\epsilon>0$ implied by the work of Ghazi, Golowich, Kumar, and Manurangsi [STOC 2021] and Lyu [STOC 2026], our bound is independent of $\epsilon$.The $2^{O(d\log d)}$ bound follows from a new inductive framework for bounding list replicability that builds on the recent notion of irreducibility from differentially private learning. The $2^{O(dv^*)}$ bound follows from a simple globally stable learner that takes the majority vote of multiple empirical risk minimizers, with stability established using tools from stable regularity.
  </details>

- **[On the Limits of Sparse Linear Model with Shuffled Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32822)**  `arXiv:2610.32822`  
  _Yan Zhang, Jiong Tuo, Meng Ding, Yuheng Ma, Di Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse linear regression is a fundamental problem in high-dimensional statistics, and its private counterparts leave a large gap between the central and the local model of differential privacy. Shuffled differential privacy (SDP) sits between the two, offering accuracy closer to the central model without the trust requirement of a central curator. This paper studies sparse linear regression under both non-interactive and interactive SDP protocols. In the non-interactive setting, any non-interactive $(\epsilon,\delta)$-SDP protocol is shown to incur an $\ell_2$-norm estimation error of $\Omega( \frac{\sqrt{dk}}{n\epsilon} )$ with dimension $d$, sparsity $k$, and $n$ users, revealing an inherent dimensionality barrier. In addition, a non-interactive algorithm is proposed that achieves an $\ell_2$-norm upper bound of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}( \sqrt{\frac{k}{n}} + \frac{d\sqrt{k}}{n\epsilon})$. To overcome this barrier, we turn to the interactive user-level setting, where each user holds $m$ samples. A two-round interactive algorithm is developed that first recovers the support through a heavy hitter step and then estimates the parameter, achieving an error of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}( \sqrt{\frac{k}{nm}} + \frac{k}{n\sqrt{m}\epsilon} )$ with no polynomial dependence on $d$. Furthermore, by extending the score attack framework to user-level data, a new lower bound $\Omega( \sqrt{\frac{k\log(d/k)}{nm}} + \frac{k\log(d/k)}{n\sqrt{m}\epsilon} )$ is established for any user-level $(\epsilon,\delta)$-DP, showing that the proposed algorithm is near-optimal with only a gap of the factor $\log(d/k)$.
  </details>

- **[CARES: Coverage-Aware Replay Exemplar Selection for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32799)**  `arXiv:2610.32799`  
  _Arundhati S. Shanbhag, Brian B. Moser, Federico Raue, Tobias C. Nauen, Andreas Dengel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Replay-based Continual Learning mitigates catastrophic forgetting by keeping a buffer of past samples, making buffer composition critical under a fixed memory budget. Recent coreset-based replay methods improve buffer quality using model-derived gradients, losses, or auxiliary optimization, but incur substantial computational overhead as memory is repeatedly updated. We propose CARES, a continual model-optimization-free approach for buffer construction that instead exploits the geometry of a frozen pretrained feature space using class-aware, density-weighted coverage. We evaluate CARES across balanced, imbalanced, and label-noisy streams under online class- and task-incremental settings. On Tiny-ImageNet, CARES is 2.3 to 8.8 times faster than existing coreset-based selectors. Although CARES achieves this speedup using no training signal from the continual learner, it ranks among the state of the art across balanced, imbalanced, and label-noisy streams under online class- and task-incremental settings, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 7.58 percentage points under class imbalance while also attaining higher average accuracy on Tiny-ImageNet. Code will be released upon publication
  </details>

- **[Deep Learning-Based Prediction of HER2 Status from Whole-Brain MRI in Breast Cancer Brain Metastases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32774)**  `arXiv:2610.32774`  
  _Sara Early, Chris Kang, Vibujithan Vigneshwaran, Kimberly Amador, Roger Y. Tsang, Nils D. Forkert_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: Human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) status is a key biomarker relevant for prognostication and treatment decision making in metastatic breast cancer. Thus, the aim of this work was to explore whether HER2 status can be predicted directly from whole-brain post-contrast T1-weighted (T1w) MRI scans in breast cancer brain metastases (BCBM) using deep learning, and whether providing explicit tumour segmentation information improves prediction.Methods: Using the publicly available BCBM-Radiogenomics dataset, we analysed 135 patients, including 70 HER2+ and 65 HER2- patients. Three approaches were implemented and evaluated: a conventional radiomics model, a simple fully convolutional network (SFCN) using T1w MRI scans alone, and an SFCN using T1w MRI scans with an expert-derived tumour mask as an additional input channel.Results: The radiomics model achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.602 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.528--0.677) while the SFCN achieved a ROC-AUC of 0.667 (95% CI, 0.63--0.71) using T1w MRI scans alone, which increased to 0.692 (95% CI, 0.58--0.81) when an explicit tumour mask was additionally provided.Conclusion: However, the modest performance across all approaches demonstrates the difficulty of predicting HER2 status from structural MRI scans alone. The difference between the segmentation-free and mask-guided SFCN models suggests that much of the predictive signal captured by the deep-learning model may be learned directly from whole-brain T1w MRI without requiring explicit tumour delineation. These findings support further investigation of segmentation-free deep-learning approaches as a potential non-invasive strategy to support HER2 status determination in BCBM.
  </details>

- **[Direction-Aware Token Credit Assignment with Reward-Model Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32772)**  `arXiv:2610.32772`  
  _Yikuan Hu, Jifeng Zhu, Minle Su, Jiaqin Yue, Gujie Shao, Yanzhe Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GRPO shares a response-level advantage across tokens, leaving unresolved how strongly each token should contribute to learning. Updates induced by the shared advantage reinforce or suppress the sampled token at each position, but these updates do not always point toward increasing reward at the token level. This motivates a work-based view of credit: just as work depends on the component of force along a displacement, token credit should reflect the local reward change along the policy update direction. We introduce Direction-Aware Token Credit Assignment (DATC), which maps frozen reward-model gradients into policy logit space and allocates weights by evaluating the alignment between the reward direction and GRPO's signed sampled-action update direction, while preserving the sign and mean of the response-level advantage. DATC outperforms GRPO in all four settings across two model families.
  </details>

- **[On the Identifiability of Hybrid Deep Generative Models: Meta-Learning as a Solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32748)**  `arXiv:2610.32748`  
  _Yubo Ye, Maryam Toloubidokhti, Sumeet Vadhavkar, Xiajun Jiang, Huafeng Liu, Linwei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The interest in leveraging physics-based inductive bias in deep learning has resulted in recent development of hybrid deep generative models (hybrid-DGMs) that integrate known physics-based mathematical expressions in neural generative models. To identify these hybrid-DGMs requires inferring parameters of the physics-based component along with their neural component. The identifiability of these hybrid-DGMs, however, has not yet been theoretically probed or established. How does the existing theory of the un-identifiability of general DGMs apply to hybrid-DGMs? What may be an effective approach to construct a hybrid-DGM with theoretically-proven identifiability? This paper provides the first theoretical probe into the identifiability of hybrid-DGMs, and presents meta-learning as a novel solution to construct identifiable hybrid-DGMs. On synthetic and real-data benchmarks, we provide strong empirical evidence for the un-identifiability of existing hybrid-DGMs using unconditional priors, and strong identifiability results of the presented meta-formulations of hybrid-DGMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Internal Discrepancy for Contrastive Learning: Generalization Bounds and Downstream Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32729)**  `arXiv:2610.32729`  
  _Jialiang Wang, Qing Ma, Xiong Zhou, Gangfeng Hu, Haoliang Li, Xianming Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive learning has emerged as a popular paradigm for training representation models. While prior studies pursue more practical and less restrictive assumptions to enhance theoretical understanding of contrastive learning, they essentially describe the interconnections among representations within the data or induced by the function class. To encapsulate these assumptions, we propose the measure of internal discrepancy that quantifies the difference between the mean representation of positive samples and latent cluster centers. We then prove an internal discrepancy-aware generalization bound for downstream supervised tasks following contrastive learning, without requiring additional assumptions. This generalization bound not only serves as a unifying framework connecting various assumptions but also motivates us to further reduce the internal discrepancy, thereby introducing the relaxed k-means loss as an approximation. Moreover, we investigate the downstream behaviors of contrastive learning from the perspective of contrastive learning-incurred supervised losses. In the presence of class imbalance, we shed light on why contrastive learning can facilitate imbalanced learning. Finally, we investigate the optimality of these incurred supervised losses, proving several intriguing properties.
  </details>

- **[PlayerEmbed: Learning Behavioral Fingerprint Representations from Raw Input Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32727)**  `arXiv:2610.32727`  
  _Tobias Schneider, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Priya Tomar, Shahzeb Qamar, Tobias Deußer, Rafet Sifa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Raw player input sequences in video games contain fine-grained behavior, but are high-dimensional and noisy, which makes large-scale analytics challenging. To address this, we introduce PlayerEmbed, a framework that learns compact and discriminative player embeddings using deep metric learning. Our approach uses a hybrid CNN-LSTM encoder trained on raw input sequences from 1,000 players. We evaluate the model on held-out sequences from these players and on sequences from 1,000 players entirely unseen during training to assess generalization to unseen identities. For player identification, a nearest-centroid classifier built from reference embeddings achieves strong performance, reaching balanced accuracies of 0.950 for players seen during training and 0.879 for players unseen during training. Systematically increasing the number of training players substantially improves generalization to unseen players, indicating a transition from simple fingerprinting to learning transferable behavioral patterns.
  </details>

- **[DT-JEPA: Learning to Predict Future Representations for Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32657)**  `arXiv:2610.32657`  
  _Changxu Ni, Yiyang Ni, Jun Li, Feiran You, Hongyang Du, Haitao Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision Transformer (DT) has demonstrated strong performance in various offline reinforcement learning (RL) tasks by conditioning action generation on expected future returns and historical trajectories. However, treating expected future returns as the condition provides an incomplete description of the future, as trajectories with different state transitions and behaviors may share similar returns. Moreover, expected future returns inherently depend on task-specific reward functions, which makes policy learning reliant on reward-labeled datasets and restricts its scalability to tasks with similar but unseen rewards. Recently, Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) have emerged as a promising paradigm of self-supervised learning-based world models, which learn predictive representations of future dynamics in latent space rather than reconstructing future states in the original space. Inspired by JEPA, we propose DT-JEPA, an offline pretraining and online fine-tuning framework that learns general knowledge from reward-free offline trajectories and achieves excellent adaptation to unseen tasks. Instead of predicting a single deterministic future embedding, we design a novel probabilistic predictor to model the conditional distribution of future representations in the latent space, thereby capturing both predictive information and uncertainty about future dynamics. Theoretically, we show that 1) the JEPA objective seeks to maximize a variational lower bound on the mutual information between historical representations and future trajectories; and 2) predictive future-conditioned policy learning admits a finite-sample performance bound with an O(N-1/4) estimation term. Experiments on D4RL benchmarks demonstrate that DT-JEPA achieves efficient adaptation across varying offline RL tasks, outperforming traditional DT-based and other offline RL methods.
  </details>

- **[WL-SeqVAE: Sequence-Based Graph Generation Using Variational Autoencoders and Weisfeiler-Leman Colors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32650)**  `arXiv:2610.32650`  
  _Iakovos Evdaimon, Giannis Nikolentzos, Achilleas Tsimichodimos, Christos Xypolopoulos, Michalis Vazirgiannis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we introduce WL-SeqVAE, a sequence-based variational autoencoder for generating graphs. The key observation behind WL-SeqVAE is that node colors induced by the Weisfeiler-Leman (WL) algorithm provide compact discrete summaries of local graph structure and can therefore serve as structural tokens for generation. Rather than learning a distribution over adjacency matrices directly, our method maps each graph to a sequence of WL colors and employs a variational autoencoder that encodes this sequence into a Gaussian latent distribution, from which an autoregressive decoder reconstructs the graph. During generation, compatibility constraints derived from the WL colors guide node and edge predictions, combining global information from the latent representation with local structural consistency. This formulation turns graph generation into a sequence generation problem and avoids directly parameterizing a quadratic adjacency matrix. We evaluate WL-SeqVAE on molecular and general graph generation tasks. Across the evaluated datasets, WL-SeqVAE is competitive with established baselines on several local structural and molecular quality metrics. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[The Standard Deviation in VICReg Buys a Safety Margin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32630)**  `arXiv:2610.32630`  
  _Hawar Dizayee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  VICReg prevents collapse with a hinge on the standard deviation of each embedding dimension, and its authors argue from the gradient that a hinge on the variance would fail. To our knowledge, this claim has not been tested experimentally. We test it in a controlled setting (ResNet-18, CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100), changing only the hinge and sweeping its weight $\mu$ over five orders of magnitude. At the default weight ($\mu = 1$), the two hinges reach the same linear-probe accuracy on CIFAR-10 (about 89.5%). Both collapse below a threshold weight, but the standard-deviation hinge's threshold is 35-130$\times$ lower. CIFAR-100 shows a consistent gap. In training, the variance hinge's gradient fades as the feature variance shrinks, while the standard-deviation hinge's does not until that variance is very small. The standard-deviation hinge therefore buys a safety margin: it keeps training at weights far below the point where the variance hinge collapses.
  </details>

- **[Interpreting Supervised Pairwise Graph Representations: Local Evidence, Confounding, and Provable Post-hoc Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32611)**  `arXiv:2610.32611`  
  _Ilya Brekin, Ariela Moldavskaya, Elina Sidorova, Ural Valeev_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised pairwise graph representations convert pairwise predictive relations into sample-specific graphs, but graph-level accuracy does not by itself show that higher-order topology carries the signal. In a controlled Transformer error-detection task, supervised pairwise graphs outperform attention-as-adjacency (ROC-AUC $0.839$ versus $0.559$; the comparable no-confidence pair is $0.687$ versus $0.559$), but their apparent advantage over model confidence disappears after controlling for sequence length, which is almost perfectly recoverable from the pooled hidden state ($R^2=0.9995$). On published HCP Emotion ensemble graphs, signed node aggregation already acts as an ensemble of supervised pairwise predictions. Edge magnitude alone is near chance; topology-aware augmentations change ROC-AUC by at most $0.004$, with only sign-separated degree showing small positive changes across all tested graph sizes, while a validation-tuned three-layer GCN provides no measurable improvement over the signed node-summary baseline. A Fisher post-hoc corrector satisfies its distribution-agnostic guarantees across the evaluated feature representations and remains stable under reduced labelled budgets; in a near-error-free fMRI calibration regime the bound correctly becomes statistically uninformative. A separate cross-attention analysis identifies a redundant reversal circuit and downstream EOS-timing and copy failures. Higher-order topology should therefore be credited only after local supervised evidence and recoverable nuisance variables have been excluded.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory Matching: Discrete Flow Matching with Structured Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32602)**  `arXiv:2610.32602`  
  _Niklas Kemper, Marten Lienen, David Lüdke, Stephan Günnemann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The natural units of structured discrete generation are often not independent single-component edits but richer actions: attaching a fragment to a molecule, inserting a node together with an incident edge, or growing a hierarchy by adding a child. Yet most existing discrete flow matching methods (DFM) are restricted to independent component-wise edits over fixed-size states. This restriction stems from endpoint conditioning: endpoints fix the source and target during training but not the action sequence between them, making tractable conditional rates hard to define for complex actions that cannot be factorized component-wise. We propose trajectory matching, which conditions the flow on a full sequence of actions connecting the endpoints. This yields principled, simulation-free training targets for user-defined action spaces, including actions that change state size, modify multiple components, or depend on earlier actions. We prove that the resulting objective recovers the marginal flow and that trajectory matching strictly subsumes factorized DFM, while additionally capturing actions with prerequisite structure such as hierarchy growth or fragment-based molecule generation. Empirically, on tree and molecular graph generation, trajectory matching with domain-aligned actions matches or surpasses factorized DFM baselines (lifting MOSES validity from 92.8% to 99.2%) and transfers without retraining to substructure extension and linking. Trajectory matching enlarges the design space of discrete flow matching by turning the action vocabulary into a modeling choice. An interactive explanation of trajectory matching is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Parameter Rebasin to Functional Transfer Across Heterogeneous Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32581)**  `arXiv:2610.32581`  
  _Filippo Rinaldi, Aniello Panariello, Giacomo Salici, Simone Magistri, Angelo Porrello, Andrew D. Bagdanov, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting pretrained Transformers to downstream tasks produces task-specific updates that are expensive to reproduce for every model variant. Reusing these updates across different depths and widths is challenging because their parameters have incompatible shapes and do not share a canonical coordinate system. We introduce Ariadne, a training-free method that transfers task-specific behavior across heterogeneous Transformers without resizing the source model or transporting its parameters. Rather than treating the source task vector, i.e. the difference in parameter space between the fine-tuned and the base models, as the object to move, Ariadne characterizes fine-tuning by the activation changes it induces at matched blocks. First, it aligns these effects to a target base through local Procrustes maps. Then, it directly synthesizes a native target update by closed-form ridge regression. Across Transformer expansion, reduction and language-model transfer, Ariadne improves target-model performance while substantially reducing the time and memory required to construct an update relative to previous cross-architecture rebasin approaches. These results show that local functional effects provide an efficient alternative to parameter transport.
  </details>

- **[Amortized Thompson Sampling and Efficient Information-Directed Sampling for Simulator-Based Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32514)**  `arXiv:2610.32514`  
  _Michal Kobiela, Michael U. Gutmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thompson sampling is a widely used strategy for sequential decision-making under uncertainty, offering a principled way to balance exploration and exploitation. This strategy involves maximizing a target objective under a single sample drawn from the current belief over unknown quantities. Conventionally, this optimization is performed once the belief has been inferred from data, and it becomes computationally burdensome when many Thompson samples are required, especially in continuous action spaces. In this work, we introduce an approach that amortizes this process ahead of time, before any data is collected, so that an approximation of the full Thompson sampling distribution is available immediately once observations arrive. We employ this approach to solve the multi-round setting, involving sequential data acquisition and Thompson-sample recommendation across rounds. Beyond accelerating Thompson sampling, our approach enables efficient estimation of expected information gain about the optimal action. This can be used to select more informative Thompson samples or to obtain maximally informative designs with optimization. Combining this estimate of expected information gain with immediate regret supports information-directed sampling (IDS), which can outperform Thompson sampling by explicitly balancing these two quantities. We demonstrate our approach on two simulator-based design problems in synthetic biology, where the simulators have intractable likelihoods.
  </details>

- **[BAARC: Uncertainty-Driven Adaptive Partial Spectral Measurement to Full Reconstruction for Material Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32505)**  `arXiv:2610.32505`  
  _Adithya Ashok Chalain Valapil, Aishwarya Venkataramanan, Joachim Denzler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spectroscopic instruments with limited wavenumber coverage require accurate scan positioning or multiple measurements to complete the material analysis. This often requires prerequisite knowledge about the material under diagnosis to measure the spectral regions with distinguishing peaks. In our study, we simulate a Bayesian Adaptive Acquisition with Reconstruction-driven Classification (BAARC) framework for Raman spectral measurements, using masked training to learn spectral features that dynamically drive laser channel selection until the material is identified. We present a jointly trained two-level framework that takes progressive partial measurements of small wavenumber patches across the complete spectral range and, at each step, first attempts to reconstruct the full spectra until the model uncertainty is low. Secondly, material classification is performed at each reconstruction step until stoppage. Uncertainty quantification during reconstruction steps helps determine the measurement position of our next patch, while combining it with classification uncertainty determines measurement termination. By closing the loop between hardware acquisition and deep probabilistic modeling, the proposed system minimizes physical measurement steps while maintaining both spectral fidelity and diagnostic accuracy. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves high-accuracy sample identification using only a fraction of full-spectrum channels, offering a scalable solution for real-time, high-throughput spectroscopic instruments.
  </details>

- **[DRIVE: Fine-tuning via Data Contribution- and Diversity-aware Weighting with Prior Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32501)**  `arXiv:2610.32501`  
  _Qing Liu, Xinrui Chen, Yi Du, Ou Wu, Weiyao Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-stage fine-tuning is pervasive, yet catastrophic forgetting persists when the source pre-training data is inaccessible. Data weighting affords a natural lever to modulate sample influence. Yet existing weighting methods lack a principled mechanism to explicitly arbitrate knowledge retention against new-task adaptation, and provide no unified differentiable objective to accommodate auxiliary signals such as sample diversity and sample characteristics. This is because current schemes privilege one goal over the other, and no explicit gradient-compatible encoding exists for such signals. To address this, we propose DRIVE, a principled and efficient weight assignment framework. It derives per-sample weights via a unified differentiable objective where three coupled signals co-evolve: Shapley additivity splits contribution into anti-forgetting and adaptation components, which are reshaped by a covariance-based diversity term and stabilized by a sample-characteristic prior. Far from a mere combination, this yields an end-to-end gradient-based scheme balancing stability against plasticity. Theoretically, we prove that DRIVE enjoys superior stability-plasticity guarantees over existing weighting schemes and enhances parameter estimation robustness. Empirically, it attains superior overall performance, faster convergence, and stronger robustness. Code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A curated multi-endpoint drug-protein bioactivity dataset with construct-aware protein targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32490)**  `arXiv:2610.32490`  
  _Lina Abou-Abbas, Nour Mhanna, Hasan Bazzi, Sleiman El Hajj, Abdulkader Helwan, Rabah Omrani, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public drug-protein bioactivity resources commonly identify targets by UniProt accession, although one accession may represent distinct experimental sequences. We present Curate-DTI, a construct-aware dataset derived from the 1 July 2026 BindingDB export, with IC50, Ki, Kd and EC50 processed independently. The release contains 2,919,717 measurements, 1,291,872 standardized ligands, 9,668 sequence-defined protein entities and 2,275,683 endpoint-specific interactions spanning 8,336 reviewed UniProt accessions. Protein entities combine the accession with the reported experimental sequence, preserving constructs that accession-only processing would merge. Across the four endpoints, 4,204 endpoint-specific ligand-accession groups showed different classification labels across sequence-defined protein entities. Exact and inequality-qualified observations provide relation-aware classification evidence, whereas regression targets use exact values only. Connected Parquet tables preserve individual measurements, standardized structures, protein sequences, aggregated interactions and available assay and source provenance. The resource supports endpoint-specific classification and regression while allowing users to select molecular representations, protein representations and evaluation splits. Preprocessing code, a column dictionary, exclusion reports and validation summaries accompany the release.
  </details>

- **[Online Continual Learning in Edge-Cloud Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32472)**  `arXiv:2610.32472`  
  _Charalampos Davalas, Dimitrios Michail, Christos Diou, Iraklis Varlamis, Konstantinos Tserpes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed machine learning models in real-world IoT and edge/cloud systems face a critical reliability bottleneck: the requirement for online adaptation to non-stationary data streams while operating without access to the full original training dataset. Performance is severely degraded when models encounter Out-of-Distribution (OOD) inputs or suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Addressing these challenges requires adaptation mechanisms capable of learning from evolving data streams while preserving previously acquired knowledge. This paper proposes an edge-cloud methodology for integrating Continual Learning (CL) with Out-of-Distribution (OOD) Detection and Concept Drift Detection in IoT applications. The methodology incorporates ODIN and ECDD as alternative mechanisms for identifying informative samples for annotation and triggering rehearsal-based model updates. Experimental results across automotive, healthcare, and agricultural datasets demonstrate the applicability of the proposed methodology and its potential to support model adaptation while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.
  </details>

- **[DualGSL: Prior-Guided Graph Structure Learning with Node-Adaptive Prediction Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32462)**  `arXiv:2610.32462`  
  _Yuxia Sun, Yutszyuk Wong, Huihong Chen, Longhao Lv, Chang-Dong Wang, Li Zeng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph structure learning (GSL) adapts propagation graphs to prediction tasks, but the relative predictive value of feature-derived priors and learned relations can vary across nodes. We propose DualGSL, a prior-guided framework for transductive node classification that learns node-specific weights for combining predictions from separate prior and task-adaptive propagation paths. The prior branch augments a feature graph with virtual class nodes to incorporate training-label information, while the structural branch combines learned affinity graphs with the feature-graph prior. The two branches share encoder parameters while preserving distinct representations and classification logits. A node-adaptive gate fuses the branch logits based on their projected representations, element-wise absolute difference, and prediction confidence. A joint objective integrates fused and branch-specific classification losses with prior-branch class-anchor alignment, coordinating graph construction, representation learning, and prediction fusion. Experiments against seven baselines on the AMG18 malware attribution dataset and the widely used 20News text classification and USPS handwritten-digit recognition benchmarks show that DualGSL achieves the highest cross-dataset average accuracy and Macro-F1 among the evaluated methods. Ablation studies on AMG18 further demonstrate the benefit of combining prior and learned propagation through logit fusion.
  </details>

- **[DIGOM: Disentangled Interaction Graphs for Outcome prediction in Multimodal healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32451)**  `arXiv:2610.32451`  
  _Hakima Laribi, Olivier Lefebvre, Dan Poenaru, Martin Vallières_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal healthcare data contain relational information that extends beyond individual patient observations. Effective clinical outcome prediction therefore requires not only integrating information from multiple modalities, but also modeling the relationships between them. Standard fusion approaches do not explicitly capture these interactions, while existing graph-based approaches typically model only a subset of them. We propose DIGOM (Disentangled Interaction Graphs for Outcome prediction in Multimodal healthcare), a graph-based framework that jointly models three complementary interaction types: Intra-patient cross-modal, Cross-patient same-modal, and Cross-patient cross-modal interactions. DIGOM learns a sparse multiplex graph with disentangled views, each encoding interactions between pairs of modalities across patients. Discrete adjacency matrices with patient-adaptive connectivity are sampled via a differentiable mechanism, enabling end-to-end learning and allowing the model to select informative relationships without imposing a fixed number of neighbors. A regularization objective maintains overall graph sparsity, while message passing integrates this relational context into patient representations for outcome prediction. We evaluate DIGOM on two multimodal healthcare datasets, MIMIC-IV and NIH-TB, across five clinical prediction tasks. DIGOM achieves the highest AUROC on all five tasks while using fewer connections than existing multi-interaction approaches. Ablation studies reveal that each interaction type's contribution varies across tasks, highlighting the need to adapt relational structures per task. DIGOM also demonstrates greater robustness to missing modalities, suggesting learned relationships provide complementary information when modalities are unavailable. Post-hoc analyses further reveal task- and outcome-specific connectivity patterns in the learned graphs.
  </details>

- **[When Does Dynamics Randomization Test Context Inference?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32414)**  `arXiv:2610.32414`  
  _Oleg Smirnov, Sofiane Ennadir, John Pertoft, Bjartur Hjaltason, Sara Karimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Environments with randomized dynamics are widely used to ask whether world models need explicit context inference. However, randomization can leave little predictive information for a context encoder to add, because distinct latent parameters may produce similar transitions at visited states, or those states may already reveal the active context. We formalize the remaining one-step information as predictive headroom and compare kernels under fixed and visited state--action distributions to distinguish changes in query coverage from changes in context weights. We analyze discrete and continuous environments and find low headroom both when kernels differ little and when visitation reduces an otherwise large discrepancy. At the same time, resetting state components increases headroom, showing that context evidence accumulates in state. We propose reporting this headroom alongside the randomized parameters, so that gains from explicit context inference can be interpreted relative to the information missing from the current state and action.
  </details>

- **[Detection and Validation of Hidden Subgroup Disparities in Clinical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32362)**  `arXiv:2610.32362`  
  _Alceu Bissoto, Trung-Dung Hoang, Tim Flühmann, Nina Weng, Susu Sun, Christian F. Baumgartner, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning (ML) models used in clinical workflows often perform systematically differently across patient groups, raising concerns about equity and patient safety. Traditional subgroup analyses can expose such disparities only when rich metadata (e.g., patient demographics) is available and captures the sources of performance variation, conditions rarely met in practice. Algorithmic subgroup discovery methods offer a solution by identifying coherent groups from learned feature representations, but because true underlying stratifications are unknown, these methods are hard to evaluate. As a result, subgroup discovery has not been systematically applied or assessed for performance monitoring. Our main objective is to evaluate algorithmic subgroup discovery for performance monitoring, assessing the discovered subgroups' performance gaps and semantic coherence.
