Investigating cardiomegaly classification in a dataset of 223,228 chest X-rays, we showed that the discovered subgroups exhibited larger performance gaps (accuracies of 0.61-0.90) than traditional metadata-based analysis (0.80-0.94) and were frequently interpretable using patient, acquisition, and model metadata. As a proof of concept, we also showed that annotations from learned subgroups can be used to mitigate the biases they expose. We conclude that subgroup discovery is a practical and effective tool to enhance the performance validation and monitoring of ML systems in healthcare.
  </details>

- **[TENET: Time-point Encoding Network for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32337)**  `arXiv:2610.32337`  
  _Dongchan Cho, Juwon Hwang, Keumyeong Kang, Jiho Han, Namsoon Jung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In unsupervised multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTSAD), local variation can inflate reconstruction and forecasting errors, making observation-space discrepancy an unreliable indicator of abnormal system behavior. Since latent-space scoring can mitigate this limitation, we reframe one-class MTSAD as current-point system-state compatibility estimation and propose TENET, the Time-point Encoding Network for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection. We define the current-point system state as a latent representation of the target time point, constructed from variable-level temporal context in the input window. TENET constructs this state through a temporal layer that retrieves window context relative to the current point, encodes time with current-point-centered embeddings, and adaptively mixes the retrieved context with the current representation. With this design, TENET treats the input window not as the object to reconstruct, forecast, summarize, or score, but as context for constructing a latent representation evaluated with a fixed standard-normal negative log-likelihood score. Across benchmark datasets, TENET achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional experiments further show that TENET remains effective under alternative one-class objectives and outperforms substitutions based on generic sequence encoders, suggesting that its advantage comes from decision-aligned representation construction rather than from a specialized scoring rule. These results indicate that one-class MTSAD can be strong when the latent representation is designed around the current time point.
  </details>

- **[When to Trust Memory: Retrieval-Guided Probabilistic Spatiotemporal Forecasting under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32329)**  `arXiv:2610.32329`  
  _Haochen Lv, Jianhao Zhang, Zhichen Lei, Yang Jiang, Xiaowei Mao, Shengnan Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatiotemporal forecasting plays an important role in real-world applications, where probabilistic approaches provide uncertainty estimates for decision-making. Real-world systems are inherently non-stationary, leading to distribution shifts between training and test data. However, existing probabilistic methods mainly focus on modeling data distributions under standard settings, while distribution shift methods often handle such shifts in a coarse manner, failing to characterize shift degree and adapt prediction strategy accordingly. As a result, they often produce miscalibrated and unreliable forecasts. To address this issue, we propose REMIND, a retrieval-guided probabilistic framework for spatiotemporal forecasting under distribution shift. REMIND leverages retrieval to estimate how well current observation are supported by historical patterns, enabling adaptive prediction under different shift regimes. It employs spatiotemporal retrieval to construct a memory bank and introduces a dual branch architecture, combining a memory-calibrated probabilistic branch for mild shifts with a deviation-aware probabilistic branch for severe shifts. A shift-adaptive gate, guided by retrieval similarity and memory dispersion, balances the two branches according to shift degree. We further design a hierarchical Gaussian mixture to capture heterogeneous uncertainty over the dual branch predictions, and introduce a memory perturbation training scheme to improve generalization. Experiments on real-world datasets with distribution shifts demonstrate that REMIND consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both forecasting accuracy and probabilistic prediction quality.
  </details>

- **[SKG in Practice: OOD Generalization Cases Across FourDomains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32314)**  `arXiv:2610.32314`  
  _Jian An, Chaoshi Wu, Xinpan Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents the engineering implementation methods of the SKG inference framework and provides complete case studies across four domains. Case 1, 2D Dissipative Mechanical System: OOD MSE is 1.69 to 4.12 times lower than the best neural baseline; the state-dependent metric yields a 10^8-order improvement under controlled conditions. Case 2, Target Detection Sim-to-Real: Real-machine mAP increases from 0.2564 to 0.4964 (+24.00 points, single-run experiment, 32 images, point estimate), and AP75 increases from 0.1736 to 0.5765 (+40.29 points), with no real-machine retraining required. Case 3, Sequence Modeling sine to square: OOD MSE is 0.0296 versus Transformer 0.0641 (mean of 5 seeds, p=0.024). Case 4, Molecular Dynamics MD17: ID@100 ADE is 0.0285 versus ESTAG 0.1467; OOD@100 is 0.0316 versus 0.1411 (single seed). For each case, we present the objective, applied SKG theory, parameter settings, baseline comparison protocol, data results, and sources of advantage. Following the cases, we provide the complete methodology: pre-validation, task invariant construction, three-stage training, residual training, and the four theorems and one proposition supporting these methods. This paper not only demonstrates effective configurations of SKG across four domains but also honestly reports the variance of single-run experiments, limitations of single-seed results, negative updates caused by parameterization mismatch, and practical experience of using engineering thresholds as diagnostic signals rather than hard constraints.
  </details>

- **[Anti-coherence Regularized Absolute Scaling for Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32255)**  `arXiv:2610.32255`  
  _Donghwan Kim, Youngwoong Jin, Hyunsoo Yoon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is recognized as a critical problem in AI safety. SCALE is an effective post-hoc OOD detection method in the activation-shaping family that requires no training data and rescales penultimate activations using a percentile-based magnitude ratio. However, this ratio is ill-defined when negative activations are present, since positive and negative contributions cancel within the underlying sum, causing SCALE to degenerate on backbones such as ViT. We address this with an Absolute Fix that computes the ratio over absolute-valued activations rather than discarding negative activations through ReLU. Building on this corrected scale, we propose Anti-coherence regularization, which penalizes the scaling factor by the fraction of a sample's max-logit contributions to the max logit that are sign-mismatched and thereby suppresses unreliable evidence for the predicted class. Despite requiring no access to training data or training labels, our method ties or outperforms activation-shaping methods that rely on training data in terms of AUROC and FPR@95 on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet under the OpenOOD v1.5 protocol.
  </details>

- **[BiLoCo: Binary Low-Rank Corrections for Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32243)**  `arXiv:2610.32243`  
  _David Jin, Beshr IslamBouli, Tarushii Goel, Han Guo, Yoon Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) to FP4 has emerged as a key technique for reducing large language model inference cost, especially on Blackwell GPUs, which offer native FP4 tensor core support. Direct PTQ to FP4, however, still leaves a noticeable accuracy gap. Recent work on SVDQuant mitigates the errors introduced through FP4 quantization for diffusion models by adding high-precision low-rank corrections obtained via SVD. However, this high-precision low-rank correction requires many bits per rank, and it runs on the same tensor cores as the main FP4 GEMM. We introduce BiLoCo, a low-rank correction method whose left and right factors are stored as binary ${\{\pm 1\}}$ sign vectors rather than as BF16 numbers. The signed format uses $16{\times}$ less storage per rank-one component, and since the correction now consists of additions/subtractions, this can be run on CUDA cores concurrently with the FP4 tensor-core GEMM. On 4B--72B LLMs, BiLoCo matches or improves memory-matched SVDQuant for PTQ. We implement BiLoCo on B300 as a fused single kernel, where the FP4 GEMM runs on tensor cores, the bit-packed correction on the CUDA cores of the same launch, and the two are combined in the GEMM epilogue. With batch size $1$, the fused kernel is at most $11\%$ slower than the FP4-only GEMM for every rank up to $512$. At rank $128$, it is at most $20\%$ slower up to batch size $8$.
  </details>

- **[Correct but Unselectable: The Hidden Interface Tax in Multi-Candidate Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32235)**  `arXiv:2610.32235`  
  _Qianli Ma, Zhiqing Tang, Fanshuai Meng, Zhi Yao, Hanshuai Cui, Weijia Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models deployed under cost, latency, or privacy constraints cannot simply switch to larger models when reasoning fails. Multi-candidate generate-then-rerank offers a natural test-time scaling route. However, more candidates do not always yield higher accuracy. We identify a previously overlooked bottleneck between generation and selection: a correct answer may appear in the candidate pool yet fail to be selected because it is incomplete, unparseable, or misaligned with the answer mode the fixed selector expects. We term this loss the hidden interface tax and formalize a coverage-conversion decomposition that distinguishes raw oracle coverage from selectable coverage, the subset of problems where a correct candidate can actually be consumed by the selector. We propose Compatibility-Aware Candidate Construction (CACC), an interface layer between the candidate source and the fixed selector that repairs fragments, aligns answer modes, and removes selector-confusing noise without retraining either component. On numeric reasoning (GSM8K, competition_math), CACC raises both oracle coverage and final accuracy. On GPQA Diamond, CACC alone improves final accuracy from 5.6% to 19.2% (+13.6 pp); combined with proposer-side strengthening it reaches 25.8% (+20.2 pp). On MMLU-Pro, CACC exposes a coverage-conversion gap: answer-mode-compatible construction raises oracle coverage, but final accuracy decreases by 0.85 pp under the original selector; adding proposer-side strengthening improves final accuracy.
  </details>

- **[MS-TTRL: Breaking the Frequency Conflict in Multiple-Solution Test-Time Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32234)**  `arXiv:2610.32234`  
  _Zhixia Zhang, Binqian Xu, Huaiyang Wang, Yikun Ban, Dianbo Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific, engineering, and mathematical reasoning tasks can admit multiple valid solutions, while exhaustive annotation of the full solution set is often prohibitively costly. Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) applies reinforcement learning to unlabeled test instances, offering a label-free approach to multiple-solution reasoning. In this setting, generation frequency serves two opposing objectives: correctness estimation favors frequent outcomes, whereas outcome-level diversity preservation favors less frequent outcomes. We term this tension the \textbf{Frequency Conflict}. To resolve this conflict, we propose MS-TTRL, which obtains frequency-independent correctness estimates through Outcome-Level Self-Verification while reserving generation frequency solely for diversity preservation. Three-State Outcome Cache and the Candidate State support verification reuse across repeated observations and confidence-aware handling of ambiguous outcomes, while Confidence-Gated Inverse Probability Scaling selectively strengthens the learning signals of rare, reliably verified outcomes. Experiments across two benchmarks, four multiple-solution tasks, and three backbone models, each evaluated with five random seeds, show that MS-TTRL improves Recovery Rate by 19.13 percentage points on average over the best-performing label-free baseline and approaches the performance of the supervised IPS-GRPO.
  </details>

- **[Principia: Test-Time Hypotheses to Principle Construction for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32195)**  `arXiv:2610.32195`  
  _Dongki Kim, Sangwoo Park, Jinheon Baek, Soyeong Jeong, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires identifying the scientific principles underlying observed phenomena to guide further investigation. As these empirical principles are often unknown in complex discovery processes, they are constructed via a hypotheses-to-principle pipeline to obtain recurring causalities from progressively accumulated observations during test-time discovery. However, constructing this knowledge requires substantial domain expertise, time, and effort, making this translation stage a bottleneck for scientific discovery. To address this limitation, we propose Principia, an LLM-based framework that constructs and continually refines scientific principles at test time. Drawing on how human researchers develop principles, Principia forms comparative causal hypotheses from related inputs and their observed outcomes, then synthesizes recurring relationships across these hypotheses into inductive principles. By applying the constructed hypotheses and inductive principles to the task, it obtains new observations, revises the hypotheses used based on these new observations, and reconstructs principles from the updated hypotheses, allowing scientific knowledge to continuously evolve throughout test-time discovery. Across benchmarks spanning molecular optimization, protein engineering, gene perturbation, and molecular property prediction, Principia outperforms baselines relying on internal knowledge, external sources, or experience summaries, while generating principles that are plausible and consistent with the scientific literature, demonstrating its effectiveness in constructing and refining task-specific hypotheses and principles at test time for scientific discovery. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Dual-Adaptive Z-Score PPO: Dynamic Clipping Using Action Confidence and Normalized Advantage Magnitude](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32194)**  `arXiv:2610.32194`  
  _Taha M. Mahmoud, Naima Kaabouch_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) remains a popular on-policy reinforcement learning algorithm owing to its stability and ease of implementation. Its clipping threshold, however, is fixed, so every training sample receives the same trust-region width regardless of how reliable the corresponding policy update is. This work examines adaptive-clipping variants of PPO in which the constant clipping parameter is replaced by sample-dependent thresholds computed from policy and return statistics. The central method, Dual-Adaptive Z-Score PPO (PPO-DAZ3), sets the clipping interval for each sample from two signals: action confidence, given by the probability of the selected action under the current policy, and update confidence, given by the normalized advantage magnitude. The rest of the standard PPO training pipeline is left unchanged. Evaluation on three classic control benchmarks (LunarLander-v3, CartPole-v1, and Acrobot-v1) indicates that adaptive clipping improves learning in some settings but not all. PPO-DAZ3 performs best on LunarLander-v3, the smooth advantage-based variant PPO-AZ3S leads on Acrobot-v1, and standard PPO stays competitive on CartPole-v1, suggesting that adaptive clipping is most beneficial on harder control tasks.
  </details>

- **[EVOLVE: Uncertainty-Aware Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Prediction with Adaptive Prototype Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32189)**  `arXiv:2610.32189`  
  _Jianxin Jin, Kai Zhao, Shiyan Hu, Yang Shu, Peng Chen, Meng Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unsupervised multivariate time series anomaly prediction (AP) aims to estimate future anomaly risks from current observations, providing early-warning signals for proactive intervention and maintenance in complex systems. Existing unsupervised AP methods follow a perturbation-based paradigm, where predefined or random perturbations are injected into historical windows to simulate abnormal future evolution. However, these methods share perturbations across different variables and contexts, and learn deterministic history-to-future evolution. In reality, anomaly evolution is variable- and context-dependent: the same perturbation may be amplified into abnormal future dynamics in one variable or context, while being absorbed as normal fluctuation in another. In addition, future evolution is not uniquely determined by a historical window, since similar histories may lead to multiple plausible trajectories, such as gradual degradation or abrupt changes. To address these problems, we propose EVOLVE, a unified framework that models unsupervised AP via variable- and context-conditioned perturbations and uncertainty-aware future reasoning. EVOLVE constructs context-specific anomaly perturbations through an adaptive anomaly prototype memory, which can dynamically update and retrieve relevant perturbations at the variable level. EVOLVE models uncertainty by sampling multiple latent historical states and decoding through complementary smooth and deviation-amplifying evolution hypotheses, and the disagreement among decoded future trajectories is used to estimate future anomaly risk. Extensive experiments on six real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that EVOLVE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art anomaly prediction baselines.
  </details>

- **[CFBrainAug: Modularity-Induced Counterfactual Brain Network Augmentation for Interpretable fMRI-based Autism Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32184)**  `arXiv:2610.32184`  
  _Yifan Qiao, Sheng Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning on resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI) brain networks is promising for diagnosing Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD), but existing methods are prone to overfitting on limited training data, and their post-hoc statistical attribution offers limited clinical interpretability. We propose CFBrainAug, a counterfactual brain network learning framework that unifies data augmentation and counterfactual explanation by learning label-altered brain networks. Unlike conventional label-preserving augmentation, CFBrainAug first retrieves a label-opposite yet topologically similar candidate and then refines it under a module-number constraint. Jointly training on factual and counterfactual networks enriches the training distribution around the decision boundary, enabling a lightweight feature-selection-based SVM to perform competitively with recent graph deep learning methods. Meanwhile, the counterfactuals provide intrinsic explanations by identifying the connectivity differences between each subject and its counterfactual. Experiments on the ABIDE I dataset show that CFBrainAug reaches an accuracy of 74.1% on the NYU site, suggesting that counterfactual augmentation can complement more complex models in small-sample brain network analysis.
  </details>

- **[Sparsity-driven emergence of functional modularity in multitask neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32153)**  `arXiv:2610.32153`  
  _Bowen Shi, Qi Ouyang, Long Qian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural networks can learn multiple tasks, but how learning organizes shared computation into specialized and reusable components remains poorly understood. In this work, we show that sparsity inducing regularization promotes functional modularity in multitask networks without predefined module boundaries. Using functional profiling in multilayer perceptrons and a lightweight transformer, we identified compact, task-selective subnetworks, termed functional tokens. Across rule-based task families, selectively retaining these tokens preserves their associated functions while reducing performance on other tasks. Such specialization develops preferentially in deeper layers during training, while earlier layers retain shared computation. Regularization strength and form shape module compactness and specialization, while task weighting alters functional allocation. Moreover, extracted functional tokens can be recombined through alternative pathways to reconstruct a learned composite computation without retraining, preserving their connectivity and learned weights. Our results suggest that sparsity-constrained optimization shapes not only parameter use but also the organization and reuse of learned computation.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking LoRA Initialization for Robust Asymmetric Learning Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32142)**  `arXiv:2610.32142`  
  _Disen Liao, Yaoliang Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is often highly sensitive to learning-rate (LR) choices. Recent empirical studies suggest that, once LRs are carefully tuned, vanilla LoRA can remain competitive with many proposed LoRA variants. This shifts the central practical question from designing ever more variants to reducing the cost and brittleness of LR selection. LoRA+ addresses this issue with asymmetric LRs, using a larger LR for the up-projection matrix $B$ than for the down-projection matrix $A$. However, the ratio $\eta_B / \eta_A$ itself becomes an additional hyperparameter: large ratios can improve adaptation, but under the standard LoRA initialization they can also cause unstable training and ratio-dependent performance collapse. We identify the source of this brittleness through a signal--noise decomposition of LoRA dynamics. Under standard \initA, the update contains an initialization-induced noise term whose magnitude can be affected by the LR for $B$, $\eta_B$, the initialization scale $\beta$, and the adapter rank $r$. In practical LLM fine-tuning, this non-vanishing noise can limit the performance gains expected from LoRA+ style asymmetric LRs. To suppress this effect, we propose \initAA, a simple initialization that scales the variance of $A$ as $\sigma_A^2 \propto n^{-2}$ while keeping $B_0=0$. Under matched rank and training time for both initializations, this reduces the initialization-noise energy by an additional factor of $1/n$, stabilizing asymmetric LoRA training while preserving zero initial adapter output. Empirically, \initAA substantially widens the stable LR region across initialization scales, adapter ranks, and LoRA update multipliers. Across tasks and models, \initAA reduces best-ratio drift, prevents the high-ratio collapse observed with standard \initA, and makes the width-based guideline $\eta_B \approx n \eta_A$ a useful starting point for finite pretrained models.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Data Schedule: Fast LLM Pretraining on Data Mixtures by Loss-Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32141)**  `arXiv:2610.32141`  
  _Jiazheng Li, Huaqing Zhang, Jing Xu, Tianle Cai, Jingzhao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often acquire specialized capabilities through successive pretraining stages, but these gains can come at the cost of forgetting earlier knowledge. In this work, we investigate whether such trade-offs can be improved by changing how strongly each domain contributes to learning while keeping its data exposure fixed. We find that, in controlled knowledge--mathematics experiments, loss reweighting produces a single checkpoint that surpasses the best knowledge and reasoning scores attained along either stage trajectory. This reveals an independent control that data-mixture ratios alone do not capture. Building on this finding, we propose LARE (Loss-Adaptive Reweighting under fixed Exposure), which automatically adjusts domain weights from running loss statistics. Motivated by a theoretical analysis of gradient noise, its normalized inverse-square-root rule balances relative loss scales without changing the sampled mixture or requiring additional runs to search for weights. Across knowledge, mathematics, and code, \Method{} improves all five evaluated benchmarks for a 1.5B model trained on 240B tokens, averaging +7.84 pp over a matched direct mixture, including gains of +17.06 pp on GSM8K and +9.00 pp on MATH. These findings demonstrate that adjusting domain loss scales can improve joint capabilities without additional domain exposure.
  </details>

- **[ReMedI: Inference-Time Refinement and Self-Distillation for Controllable Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32103)**  `arXiv:2610.32103`  
  _Jia Yi Zhang, Shivanshu Shekhar, Allison McLevige, Tong Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In drug development, lead optimization must improve competing properties, such as increasing permeability and reducing toxicity, while limiting scaffold changes that could compromise the lead's potency. RL-based post-training, such as C-MORAL, addresses this problem by fine-tuning molecular language models to directly optimize molecular leads for objectives of interest, which relies on expensive task-specific datasets and faces challenges in achieving multi-objective optimization (MOO). We argue a pre-trained model may already contain useful molecular edits that zero-shot generation cannot efficiently reach. Motivated by the limited search capabilities of zero-shot generation, we develop an efficient Metropolis-Hastings search strategy to better explore the proposal distribution. We propose ReMedI, which uses the molecular language model as a base proposer for inference-time alignment. Given a lead and a set of multi-objective tasks, the model proposes edits conditioned on the current molecule, while Metropolis--Hastings refinement guides sequential edits under an objective for property improvement, preservation, and lead similarity. On both C-MuMOInstruct and SMDD benchmarks, refinement improves frozen base models across three backbones, reaching significantly higher multi-objective optimization success rates compared to baselines. These search traces can then distilled into the base model which improves zero-shot generation and subsequent search using approximately 7.8 times less training molecular leads than that of C-MORAL. ReMedI achieves approximately 2.8 times the strict-success coverage of RL baseline C-MORAL, including on out-of-distribution tasks, with matched lead-relative similarity.
  </details>

- **[PRISM: Promoting Sampling Diversity of Large Language Models with Implicit Stochastic Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32101)**  `arXiv:2610.32101`  
  _Qixin Xu, Zihan Zhu, Xiaojiang Liu, Bohan Lyu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) exhibit mode collapse, where their output distribution concentrates on familiar semantic modes. When diversity disappears, repeated sampling becomes semantic repetition rather than discovery, making rare but stronger solutions unlikely to surface and leaving self-evolution and continual learning difficult to sustain. Most remedies intervene only at inference time, shaping sam- ples without making diversity a model capability. We propose PRISM, a training-time framework that internalizes diversity within the model. PRISM combines (i) submodular curation that pairs each prompt with diverse-yet-correct multi-teacher solutions, and (ii) stochastic embedding modulation, which turns special-token perturbations into implicit signals for sampling distinct outputs. A theoretical analysis establishes the stability of this mechanism. On WritingPrompts and BookMIA, PRISM moves the quality-diversity frontier outward, achieving the best generation quality in all model-benchmark settings while maintaining the strongest or near-strongest semantic diversity across two open-weight model families. It also improves sampling efficiency on math and code benchmarks. Beyond inference, PRISM yields a stronger foundation for reinforcement learning, sustaining diverse rollouts and mitigating the diversity collapse commonly observed during RL training.
  </details>

- **[Controlled Evaluation of EEG Foundation Models: Random Initialisation, Band-Power and Validation-Selected Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32067)**  `arXiv:2610.32067`  
  _Mingyu Jeon, Suwan Cho, Jae Young Suh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EEG foundation models report downstream gains, yet their protocols seldom include three controls. These are the same encoder untrained, spectral features fitted on the same data, and a layer picked without looking at test scores. We therefore evaluate five publicly released EEG foundation models and an in-house JEPA-style model under one protocol with all three controls and several seeds, on five benchmarks and four added task families. The controls change the reading in three ways. First, picking the probed layer by its test score raises the layer-wise peak by an average of +0.034 and reverses the peak-versus-output gap in 9 of 36 cells. Second, with the layer picked on validation, random initialisation matches or beats pretraining for half of the models on sleep and emotion, and no model beats a five-band log-power baseline on emotion or motor imagery. Third, after full fine-tuning only 5 of 30 pairs gain beyond twice their seed spread, and the one clear loss of -0.290 AUROC persists under matched learning rates. From these results we draw four reporting practices, and we release every cell for re-analysis.
  </details>

- **[GS-MoE: Training-Free MoE Compression via Groupwise Subspace Sharing and Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32009)**  `arXiv:2610.32009`  
  _Carson Cary, Yukai Jin, Yihao Huang, Zhankai Ye, Fangyi Li, Ting Hua, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce computation through sparse activation, but storing their expert parameters remains a major memory bottleneck. We introduce GS-MoE, a training-free compression framework that addresses redundancy across and within experts through two compression axes: shared-subspace rank and expert intermediate width. Groupwise low-rank representations capture shared structure across experts while retaining expert-specific projections, and Nyström-based neuron compression reduces intermediate width within each expert. For fixed shared subspaces, we derive per-expert reconstruction-error reductions from the reconstruction objective and use them to guide expert grouping. Budgets are allocated hierarchically using a gradient-free layer-importance score to guide the allocation of rank and width budgets across layers, which eigenvalues and leverage then distribute as non-uniform ranks across groups and intermediate widths across experts. Experiments demonstrate the complementary benefits of compression along both axes, particularly at high compression rates on fine-grained MoE models. On DeepSeek-MoE-16B-Base, at a 60% reduction in total model parameters, GS-MoE retains approximately 90% of the uncompressed model's average reasoning performance, compared with 73% for D$^2$-MoE, while achieving 20% lower out-of-domain perplexity. We provide a code implementation at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[REDAR: Reinforcement-Driven Adaptive Regularisation for Class Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31991)**  `arXiv:2610.31991`  
  _Akila Ayanthi, Darshana Priyasad, Tharindu Fernando, Sridha Sridharan, Clinton Fookes, Peyman Moghadam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Regularisation-based class-incremental learning (CIL) methods control the stability-plasticity trade-off through fixed coefficients tuned offline, independently of the continual learning objective and blind to phase-specific variations in data volume, task difficulty, and class similarity. We argue this is a fundamental mismatch and propose REDAR: Reinforcement-Driven Adaptive Regularisation for Class Incremental Learning, which treats regularisation strength selection as a sequential decision problem. At each incremental phase, a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) agent observes a compact state of current performance metrics and incoming data statistics, and outputs the distillation coefficients for that phase. To respect CIL's constraint on historical data access, the policy is trained entirely on pseudo-CIL tasks constructed from the initial phase, then deployed on the incremental sequence without further adaptation. Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet demonstrate that REDAR reduces forgetting and improves forward transfer over fixed and heuristic baselines, and that policies trained on short pseudo-task horizons transfer effectively to longer incremental runs.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Heuristics and Commercial Navigation: An AI-Augmented Decision-Support for Ambulance Travel Time Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31968)**  `arXiv:2610.31968`  
  _Muhammad Adil Abid, Johan Holmgren, Fabian Lorig, Jesper Petersson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate ambulance travel time estimation is critical for emergency medical dispatch, yet existing decision-support practices often rely on static lookup tables or simplified assumptions regarding how ambulance travel time differs from ordinary passenger-car travel. For instance, they assume ambulances simply travel a fixed percentage (e.g., 5%) faster than ordinary passenger cars. However, such assumptions are rarely validated using real ambulance operational data and may fail to capture the effects of trip priority, temporal context, and origin-destination. We address these limitations using historical ambulance trips from the Skåne region in Sweden. Our analysis reveals that ambulance travel times vary substantially by hour, day, and trip priority, and that commercial navigation systems fail to capture emergency-specific dynamics. We contribute a machine learning system to more accurately estimate ambulance travel time. To make the system interpretable, we explored SHapley Additive exPlanations and permutation importance techniques to ensure that our system captures operationally meaningful patterns.
  </details>

- **[Towards Trustworthy AI-Enabled Emergency Medical Dispatch: Uncertainty-Aware Ambulance Travel Time Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31944)**  `arXiv:2610.31944`  
  _Muhammad Adil Abid, Johan Holmgren, Fabian Lorig, Jesper Petersson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emergency medical dispatch increasingly relies on AI-enabled decision support systems when making ambulance dispatch decisions. In this process, accurate ambulance travel time estimates are essential for timely resource allocation and prehospital care. However, existing ambulance travel time estimation approaches typically produce only single point predictions. Consequently, they do not indicate how reliable an individual estimate is or how much uncertainty it carries. This limitation is particularly problematic in safety-critical emergency medical service dispatch operations, where trips with similar expected travel times may exhibit substantially different levels of uncertainty. Furthermore, real-world ambulance trip logs may contain domain-specific artefacts, such as co-located healthcare facilities and physically implausible travel speeds. These artefacts can degrade point prediction accuracy and affect the prediction task on which subsequent uncertainty estimation depends. To address these challenges, we propose an uncertainty-aware methodology for ambulance travel time estimation and evaluate it through a retrospective real-world scenario study using two years of ambulance trip data from the Skåne region in Sweden. The proposed methodology moves beyond average-case point predictions by generating context-adaptive prediction intervals with stable empirical coverage across the evaluated operationally important contexts. The resulting prediction intervals provide explicit information about predictive uncertainty that can support more transparent and risk-sensitive ambulance dispatch decision-making.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Local Accuracy: Benchmarking the Rollout Reliability of PDE Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31935)**  `arXiv:2610.31935`  
  _Jaewan Park, Seid Koric, Giri Krishnan, Sudhakar Pamidighantam, Surresh Marru_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  PDE foundation models enable data-efficient adaptation but are deployed autoregressively, so short-horizon accuracy need not imply reliable simulation. We ask whether the short-horizon advantage of a foundation-model recipe persists when its predictions are repeatedly fed back as inputs for a long horizon. We define error amplification as G=E(H_f)/E(H_s), which measures full-horizon error relative to short-horizon error. We evaluate seven models on seven two-dimensional time-dependent PDE datasets: scratch-trained FNO and DeepONet; fine-tuned DPOT, GPhyT, Poseidon, and Walrus; and frozen in-context VICON. We report VRMSE, relative L_2, and relative MAE. Six datasets provide paired short- and full-horizon scores, while CE-RM contributes short-rollout sweeps only. At least one fine-tuned foundation model outperforms both scratch baselines in 26 of 30 short-rollout dataset--budget cells under every metric. Nevertheless, full-horizon VRMSE exceeds short-horizon VRMSE in 140 of 144 paired trained configurations, with a median error amplification of $2.04$. The count below VRMSE one falls from 104 to 53, and 12 full rollouts exceed ten although none does at the short horizon. Walrus achieves the lowest full-horizon VRMSE in 16 of 24 cells, yet its median error amplification is $2.70$. Relative L_2 and relative MAE show the same qualitative pattern. These results show that favorable short-horizon rankings can coexist with substantial error amplification. PDE foundation-model benchmarks should therefore report horizon-resolved absolute errors, amplification statistics, and failure incidence rather than a single local forecast score.
  </details>

- **[Every Weight Is a Path of Shared Discrete Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31931)**  `arXiv:2610.31931`  
  _Ammar Ali, Stamatis Lefkimmiatis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce TWIG (Tree-structured Weights with Inherited Gates), a representation in which every weight of a neural network is the end of a path of small discrete decisions: a sign, a shift by a power of two, a zero gate, an index into a shared table. Weights that belong together share the upper part of their paths, and each decision is inherited by every weight below it, so it is stored once and applied once. This simple change has three consequences. Any trained network can be written as a TWIG, and when the decisions are powers of two its weight layers become circuits made almost entirely of wires and adders, suited to chips whose weights are fixed at fabrication. Pruning, quantization, low-rank factorization, codebooks and weight sharing become edits of one object, so a single optimizer can choose among all of them for a whole network. And choosing the decisions is the ground-state problem of a higher-order Boltzmann machine whose interactions follow the paths of the tree, the problem class that annealers and quantum optimizers are built to solve. Today these questions are handled apart: each compression method brings its own format and solver, a circuit with fixed weights needs a dedicated constant multiplier for every weight, and discrete solvers reach only narrow subproblems such as rounding. Weight formats have long been moving toward shared decisions, through group scales, shared exponents, microexponents and sparse weights; TWIG takes this trend to its end, and every common format is a shallow TWIG at no extra cost. On five weight matrices of a large language model, a flat tree whose signed power-of-two terms are chosen per weight for circuit cost, with no group scales, needs 9--15\% fewer additions than GPTQ at equal layer error at 3- and 4-bit accuracy on typical matrices, and 20--55\% fewer on matrices with strong outliers.
  </details>

- **[Topological Equivalence of Spectral and Density Cluster Hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31909)**  `arXiv:2610.31909`  
  _Sajjad Hashemian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We compare two canonical ways to recover hierarchical clustering from an underlying density $f$: the \emph{density cluster tree} given by connected components of superlevel sets $\{f\ge\lambda\}$, and a \emph{spectral hierarchy} obtained by applying single linkage to diffusion distances induced by a Laplacian operator. We give conditions under which, at an appropriate diffusion time, the diffusion hierarchy is \emph{topologically equivalent} to the density cluster tree up to a monotone rescaling of heights. It further shows that a $k$-NN graph implementation inherits this equivalence from uniform approximation of diffusion distances, yielding a finite-sample reduction from spectral graph constructions to density-level-set topology.
  </details>

- **[Learning Structured Sparsity for Large Models: Bridging Model Compressibility and Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31897)**  `arXiv:2610.31897`  
  _Tianxiang Xia, Kai Lion, Niao He, Michael Muehlebach, Melanie Zeilinger, Hao Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling laws continue to reward increases in model scale, while the resulting computational and memory demands drive greater reliance on cloud infrastructure for inference. Yet unreliable network connectivity, communication delays, and privacy requirements can make such reliance impractical, motivating local execution on edge devices. Local deployment requires models to fit within limited memory and execute efficiently with the available computational resources. Recent work has shown that learning sparse-plus-low-rank representations enables elastic compression of a single checkpoint to multiple memory budgets. However, fewer parameters do not necessarily translate into faster inference. Indexing overhead and irregular memory access in unstructured sparse components can offset computational savings. To address this critical deployment barrier, we introduce S-SALAAD, a unified framework that jointly learns the model weights and their low-rank and structured sparse components during training. The framework supports a family of hardware-compatible structured sparsity patterns. It couples this structure learning with budget-aware compression and dedicated CPU and GPU kernels that exploit the learned structure for acceleration, enabling efficient inference from a single checkpoint across memory budgets. Experiments on Llama2 and Qwen3 models, covering both pretraining and distillation, show that S-SALAAD advances the empirical Pareto frontier in perplexity, memory, and inference latency across multiple hardware platforms relative to the evaluated baselines. These results show that structured learning can translate elastic compressibility into reduced memory requirements and lower inference latency, enabling practical deployment of large models on edge devices.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Transformer Energy-based Models For High Quality Image Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31889)**  `arXiv:2610.31889`  
  _Jungsoo Lee, Alexi Gladstone, Yilun Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Energy-Based Models (EBMs) are a flexible class of generative models that decouple probabilistic modeling from the generation process. This decoupling directly enables compositional generation and inference-time scaling, where sample quality can be refined simply by increasing sampling steps. However, severe challenges in high-dimensional sampling and training instabilities with deep architectures have restricted EBMs to low-dimensional regimes. To resolve these bottlenecks, we introduce the Transformer Energy-Based Model (TEM), a scalable transformer energy function operating entirely within a latent space. On top of this backbone, we extract energies at multiple layers and sum them, composing complementary constraints from local texture to global semantics. Furthermore, two components make this high-quality at scale: Positive Representation Alignment (PRA), which supervises TEM's intermediate representations with a vision foundation model, and advanced sampling strategies that improve sample quality at inference. TEM-XL achieves an FID-50K of 3.82 on class-conditional ImageNet 256$\times$256 without classifier-free guidance, showing that a contrastive EBM can generate high-fidelity images. On ImageNet 512$\times$512, TEM achieves an FID-50K of 8.24, demonstrating that the approach scales to higher resolution. We further validate the core promises of EBMs: compositional generation, zero-shot inpainting, and out-of-distribution detection.
  </details>

- **[Effectiveness of Curriculum Learning Depends on Reward Sparsity and Competing Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31884)**  `arXiv:2610.31884`  
  _John J. Vastola, Ann Huang, Satpreet H. Singh, Samuel J. Gershman, Kanaka Rajan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While animals and people tend to learn a task more quickly and reliably when they first train on simpler versions of it, curriculum learning's effectiveness in artificial settings varies, and appears substantially greater in reinforcement learning than in supervised learning. In this work, we construct and analyze a minimal model of policy learning to better understand why. We consider an open-loop, episodic target interception task whose parameters---especially how close the agent must be to the target to receive an informative reward signal---can be chosen so that the agent either receives informative rewards frequently ('dense' rewards) or rarely ('sparse' rewards). We mathematically and numerically analyze the dynamics of REINFORCE-based policy learning in both cases. In the dense reward case, curricula have only marginal benefits; in the sparse case, curricula can be required for learning to occur at all. We find that this is especially true when the agent has the conflicting goals of minimizing effort and accumulating high reward, since this can produce distinct reward landscape optima which compete with one another. Our characterization of useful curricula in this setting indicates that they address two issues: they (i) make rewards less sparse to speed up learning, and (ii) shape the reward landscape to steer agents away from bad local optima.
  </details>

- **[Forecasting Associations, Not Interventions: A Preregistered Causal Audit of Covariate-Aware Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31869)**  `arXiv:2610.31869`  
  _Zong-Han Bai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Covariate-aware time series foundation models (TSFMs) accept a future path for a control variable and return a forecast, inviting what-if queries. The query is interventional; the models are trained on observational data. We determine whether their answers are the conditional slope $\partial E[Y|X]$ or the interventional effect $\partial E[Y|do(X)]$, at three levels. Theory: a Bayes-optimal observational predictor answers interventional queries associationally unless the adjustment set is available and used; for in-context least squares we derive the resulting confounding gap -- a model's effect estimate under an observational minus under a randomized context -- in closed form, a parameter-free reference line. Behaviour: on a preregistered grid with exact counterfactual ground truth, Chronos-2, TiRex-2, TabPFN-TS and TimesFM-2.5 all track this line ($R^2$ 0.90-1.00) and respond more confounded than regression itself (slopes 1.3-1.8), on linear and nonlinear structural models. Pairs of models with identical observational laws prove this is not an identifiability limit: when the effect is identifiable, least squares recovers 100% of the true effect difference, Chronos-2 75%, TiRex-2 34%. Representation: linear probes decode the data-generating regime (94-96%) and the true effect ($R^2$ 0.7-0.9) from embeddings that the forecasts ignore. On two physical systems with randomized actuators, pretrained models under-respond by 2-5x and ignore in-window confounding. Minutes of interventional post-training on 3,000 synthetic series cut the gap by up to 83% and transfer outside the training prior. Its forecasting cost is a replay-distribution effect: replaying the model's own pretraining prior cuts it from +47% to +10% MASE, and with low-rank adaptation yields a recipe meeting the preregistered criterion over three seeds (52-61% gap reduction at +1-3% cost) while improving the DoTime benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Cautious Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31855)**  `arXiv:2610.31855`  
  _Damian Hattler, Qiang Liu, Nils Thuerey_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial differential equations (PDEs) are indispensable tools for modeling complex phenomena in physics and engineering. Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) have emerged as a powerful alternative to traditional numerical solvers by directly incorporating physical constraints into neural network loss functions. Despite their advantages, PINN training requires multi-objective optimization, which often leads to significant gradient conflicts among boundary, initial, and residual loss terms. The Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient (ConFIG) method addresses these conflicts; however, integrating ConFIG with adaptive optimizers such as Adam introduces a critical vulnerability: the conflict-free direction generated by ConFIG is further processed by the optimizer, and the resulting weight update may no longer be conflict-free with respect to the task gradients. To address this issue, Cautious ConFIG is proposed as a concise three-line extension to the ConFIG algorithm that empirically restores the conflict-freeness guarantee prior to the weight update. The method is evaluated on three PINN benchmarks: the 1D Viscous Burgers' equation, the 1D Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the 2D Kovasznay flow. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over the standard ConFIG baseline. Additionally, the stabilizing effect of cautious masking and its reaction to conflict severity, task number, and optimizer lag are investigated using a simple toy system.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Multi-View Fusion for Urban Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31850)**  `arXiv:2610.31850`  
  _Sean Bin Yang, Zongyi Xu, Jilin Hu, Ying Sun, Kristian Torp, Bin Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban region representation learning encodes heterogeneous urban signals, such as mobility flows, points of interest, and land-use patterns, into unified embeddings for downstream tasks, including check-in prediction, crime forecasting, and service demand estimation. Existing approaches rely on globally shared fusion and joint optimization, implicitly assuming uniform modality importance and naturally balanced multi-objective training. However, urban data is inherently heterogeneous. Our empirical analysis reveals two structural issues: (i) modality utility varies markedly across regions, causing static fusion to overemphasize signals from certain modalities; and (ii) heterogeneous objectives exhibit persistent gradient imbalance, causing representational bias and unstable training. We present PRISM, a self-adjusting framework for adaptive multi-view learning. PRISM adapts sparse MoE routing to learn region-specific view allocations, while a PID (Proportional Integral Derivative)-inspired regulation mechanism mitigates gradient conflicts during optimization. Experiments on three large-scale urban datasets show that PRISM achieves competitive performance compared with strong multi-view baselines, while providing more stable optimization behavior and more balanced region representations.
  </details>

- **[Speculative Diffusion is a Tale of Two Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31819)**  `arXiv:2610.31819`  
  _Aaron Zorel, Guy Van den Broeck, Aditya Grover, Daniel Israel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding is a technique that accelerates the inference speed of autoregressive large language models (LLMs) by sampling from a faster, lower quality draft model. Traditionally, a fundamental characteristic of speculative decoding is that it is lossless with respect to the verifier distribution, guaranteeing speed without sacrificing quality. This differs from recently developed diffusion LLMs (dLLMs), which are notably able to achieve substantial speedups by degrading the quality of their outputs with less diffusion effort. Because diffusion models are able to sample faster than autoregressive models by generating tokens in parallel, they are considered strong candidates for draft models in speculative diffusion. While it is widely understood that diffusion models can sacrifice quality for speed, speculative decoding itself can also be generalized to generate lossy samples faster using more lenient acceptance criteria with respect to the verifier distribution. Presented with the two parameters of diffusion effort and speculative decoding leniency, this paper explores the full achievable Pareto frontier via joint variations of these parameters. Our experiments show that jointly varying diffusion effort and acceptance leniency can extend the speed-quality Pareto frontier over single step diffusion drafting.
  </details>

- **[Nested Sparsity for Scalable Pretraining and Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31818)**  `arXiv:2610.31818`  
  _Tianzhe Chu, Druv Pai, Yi Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-experts (MoE) transformers, the standard backbone for modern large-scale deep learning, employ conditional computation: only a sparse subset of model weights is used in each forward pass. In MoE transformers, this sparsity arises in the MoE operator, which generalizes the multi-layer perceptron in the classical transformer. In this work, we explore an alternative mechanism for conditional computation that analogously generalizes the attention operator. The sparsity induced by this mechanism naturally nests (i.e., hierarchically composes) with the sparsity induced by the MoE operator. We show that a transformer employing this nested sparsity mechanism, which we call Nestor, performs better than a conventional MoE transformer at equal compute and equal sparsity in language model pretraining. We also show that Nestor is well-suited for continual pretraining: it can be adapted to longer contexts more effectively than conventional MoEs, as well as adapt more efficiently to new domains while forgetting less on old domains. Furthermore, we demonstrate that these benefits persist as model size, dataset size, and context length grow. Our results demonstrate the potential of alternative conditional computation mechanisms for deep neural network architectures. Code and checkpoints are available on GitHub atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PaperScorer: How Predictable Is Paper Acceptance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31791)**  `arXiv:2610.31791`  
  _Sachin Konan, Jonathan Liu, Zhuang Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated research is largely evaluated by benchmark performance, but the value of research rarely reduces to a single score. Humans judge a work's holistic contribution through peer review, which is hard to model because it is noisy and depends on many factors. However, what if, given only the paper, we could predict its peer-review decision directly? We introduce PaperScorer, a family of models that predict a paper's accept or reject decision from its extracted text or page screenshots, training only on the decision after removing author identity and formatting shortcuts. Vision models are the most predictive (68% accuracy on ICLR papers and 77% on ML papers from arXiv), beating text-only and frontier reasoning models. Unlike plain text, page images retain figures, layout, and presentation. This signal is robust, surviving smaller models and even compressing the whole paper into a single image. But accurate predictions are not evenly spread. PaperScorer is strongest on the lowest and highest rated papers, while papers rated in the middle stay unpredictable for our models and frontier systems alike. Additionally, its confidence is calibrated, so its most confident predictions are also its most accurate. Overall, PaperScorer shows how much of the decision a paper carries on its own, marking a first step toward AI-driven research evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarks Are Surprisingly Compaction-Friendly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31778)**  `arXiv:2610.31778`  
  _Jeanne Salle, Hardik Bhatnagar, Matthias Bethge, Sahar Abdelnabi, Maksym Andriushchenko_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long horizon agentic tasks are now pushing LLM agents past their context window. To continue the task, the agent scaffold has to free up the context window, a process known as context compaction. Compaction is generally lossy by design: agents can silently lose task constraints, instructions, or their reasoning behind earlier decisions. Although compaction is a standard component of coding agent scaffolds, it has not been studied systematically with frontier models on realistic, long horizon agentic tasks. Previous work evaluate smaller open weight models on toy or synthetic trajectories. We fix the agent scaffold (OpenCode), implement five compaction strategies inside it, and evaluate three frontier models on three long horizon benchmarks (PaperBench, ProgramBench, PostTrainBench). For the two 1M context models, the compaction threshold does not measurably affect the task performance, whereas GPT-5.3-Codex, the model with the smallest context window, performs worse at lower thresholds on PaperBench and, less clearly, on PostTrainBench. No compaction method outperforms default self-summarization, and methods that discard more content cost some models up to 20 points while leaving other models the same. Compaction can, however, silently remove task constraints: on ProgramBench, the first compaction summary already drops about one third of the task's anticheating rules on average, and this rises monotonically with the number of compactions. On real user-agent sessions from SWE-chat, instructions about how the agent should work and what it should not do are dropped around 6 times more than specifications of what to build. We argue that current benchmarks are compaction-friendly: i.e., the objective is fixed and the agent's progress stays on disk, so most of what compaction discards can be recovered, except constraints that don't exist anywhere else.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Omni-modal Large Language Model Inference via Per-Query Modality Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31768)**  `arXiv:2610.31768`  
  _Shuqi Liu, Yuzhou Cao, Luke Ong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) can jointly process a broad range of modalities, such as text, audio, and video, enabling stronger multimodal understanding and reasoning capabilities, but often at substantial inference cost. We observe that the benefit of additional modalities varies across queries, indicating that a fixed modality configuration can be inefficient. Motivated by this, we study query-wise modality selection, which keeps the underlying Omni-LLM fixed while adaptively selecting which modalities to provide for each query. We formulate the problem as an accuracy-cost optimization problem and develop a cost-sensitive learning approach for modality selection. We evaluate our approach on WorldSense using Qwen2.5-Omni and Qwen3-Omni under three inference-cost measures: input tokens, latency, and monetary cost. Across both models, query-wise modality selection consistently improves the accuracy-cost trade-off and can even outperform the standard setting in which all available modalities are provided to the Omni-LLM. For Qwen2.5-Omni, our cost-sensitive approach matches or exceeds this full-modality accuracy while requiring only approximately 41% of the input tokens, 39% of the latency, and 61% of the monetary cost.
  </details>

- **[When and How Does Muon Precondition? The Role of Structured Gradient Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31728)**  `arXiv:2610.31728`  
  _Xiaoran Chen, Binghui Li, Liwei Wang, Pinyan Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has become a popular optimizer for large language model training, yet its theoretical foundations remain poorly understood. In this work, we study how gradient noise geometry shapes Muon's preconditioning benefits. We first show, theoretically and empirically, that under the classical isotropic noise assumption, Muon offers no acceleration beyond a rescaling of the learning rate. This motivates us to investigate structured noise arising from mini-batch sampling in associative memory with heterogeneous task frequencies. We establish an exact balancing threshold for two tasks and extend the analysis to multiple tasks, revealing how task coverage and gradient reliability jointly shape learning. Our analysis shows that Muon can soften the dependence on task frequency from linear to square-root, helping balance the learning of frequent and rare tasks. We further identify rotation-induced negative transfer in SignSGD that Muon avoids, and explain how momentum reduces sampling noise while tracking changing gradients. Controlled numerical experiments validate these mechanisms, and a preliminary LLM pretraining study illustrates the practical promise of fast-slow momentum.
  </details>

- **[End-to-End Differentiable Diffusion Conditioning for Physics-Informed Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31721)**  `arXiv:2610.31721`  
  _Ricardo Luna Gutierrez, Vineet Gundecha, Rahman Ejaz, Varchas Gopalaswamy, Riccardo Betti, Sahand Ghorbanpour, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing high-dimensional physical systems under expensive evaluations and complex constraints is a central challenge in science and engineering. In offline model-based optimization (MBO), methods must improve designs using only a fixed dataset and no test-time access to the true objective. However, existing methods typically suffer from conservatism, unconstrained surrogate exploitation, or function as static inference-time samplers that cannot refine candidates against precise physical objectives. In this paper, we propose Differentiable Diffusion Conditioning (D2C) for offline MBO, which leverages the fact that deterministic sampling makes the reverse denoising chain differentiable with respect to the conditioning signal. D2C turns a frozen conditional diffusion model into an end-to-end gradient-based optimizer by directly optimizing conditioning variables at test time via gradients from frozen surrogate objectives and physics-informed penalties backpropagated through the reverse chain. We implement this idea in a composed proposer-evaluator framework and evaluate it on laser pulse optimization for inertial confinement fusion, sustainable data-center workload scheduling, and four Design-Bench tasks. Our method achieves the strongest results on the two real-world tasks and the best average rank across four Design-Bench tasks.
  </details>

- **[Towards Adversarially Robust Multigraph Neural Networks for Financial Crime Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31718)**  `arXiv:2610.31718`  
  _Dennis Heijmans, H. Çağrı Bilgi, Rui Wang, Kubilay Atasu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial transaction networks are naturally represented as multigraphs, where repeated transactions between the same pair of accounts are modeled as distinct edges. Recently proposed multigraph neural network architectures leverage this structure explicitly to achieve a strong performance on anti-money laundering (AML) tasks. However, their robustness to adversaries that manipulate transaction structures to evade detection remains largely unexplored. We present the first scalable framework for structural attacks and adversarial training of multigraph neural networks using node-edge incidence matrices, adapting projected gradient descent and projected randomized block coordinate descent methods to optimize structural perturbations over such matrices. We further introduce a cycle-based regularizer that penalizes changes in the global counts of directed 2- and 3-cycles, providing a structural measure of attack plausibility. Experiments on synthetic pattern-detection and realistic AML datasets show that our learned attacks substantially outperform non-learnable perturbation baselines in reducing detection performance (PR-AUC), while adversarial training consistently improves robustness against these attacks. These results demonstrate that multigraph neural networks are vulnerable to learned structural attacks and that adversarial training provides an effective defense. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Collaborative In-Context Learning for Indoor Radio Map Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31702)**  `arXiv:2610.31702`  
  _Yuanzhe Peng, Jie Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indoor radio map estimation (RME) is essential for wireless applications such as coverage planning, localization, and network deployment. However, most existing methods follow a supervised learning paradigm that requires costly labeled data and task-specific model training. Moreover, indoor environments involve privacy-sensitive information, such as building layouts and structures, that often cannot be centralized. To address these challenges, we make the first attempt to formulate indoor RME as a collaborative in-context learning (ICL) problem. We propose Poly-Ripple, a framework in which multiple clients perform ICL using large autoregressive vision models (LAVMs) without model updates. Each client is prompted by its private demonstrations and predicts the radio map for the same scene query. To capture the causal structure of wireless propagation outward from the transmitter (Tx), we adopt ripple-ordered autoregressive modeling. We further introduce importance-aware aggregation to exploit heterogeneous contextual knowledge distributed across clients under the context length constraint. We provide theoretical analysis and conduct extensive experiments on a public indoor RME benchmark. The experimental results show that Poly-Ripple consistently outperforms representative ICL baselines, while aligning with our theoretical analysis.
  </details>

- **[Harmful Responses Are Jailbreak Templates: Transferring Autoregressive Jailbreaks to Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31331)**  `arXiv:2610.31331`  
  _Jinwei Gan, Zifeng Cheng, Zhiwei Jiang, Ran Sun, Shiping Ge, Qing Gu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) enable flexible masked generation through bidirectional context modeling, but this capability also introduces a distinct jailbreak attack surface. We find that a harmful response to a given query naturally serves as an effective jailbreak template: simply masking a subset of its tokens can induce a dLLM to reconstruct harmful content. Based on this observation, we propose RMT-Jailbreak, a general framework that transfers existing autoregressive (AR) jailbreak attacks to dLLMs through their induced harmful responses. RMT-Jailbreak converts an AR-generated harmful response into a high-mask-ratio template using uniform token retention, and further applies prompt-based safety filtering to mask any remaining visible harmful content. The refined template is then provided to the target dLLM for bidirectional reconstruction. Extensive experiments across multiple AR jailbreak methods and target dLLMs demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of RMT-Jailbreak, revealing strong cross-paradigm transferability of jailbreak attacks.
  </details>

- **[The Value of System 1 Models in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31315)**  `arXiv:2610.31315`  
  _Xiufeng Yang, Konstantinos Vrettos, Zeineb Ben Chaaben, Fabian Drexel, Jannik Lübberstedt, Johannes Moll, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many clinical decisions are a choice among a few fixed options, taken quickly and in large numbers. Such decisions also need an uncertainty estimate to know when to defer to a clinician. Generative language models answer slowly in free text and state their confidence poorly. In contrast, System 1 models answer zero-shot with a probability for each answer. However, their performance across clinical tasks is unknown. Four System 1 models, two proprietary and two with open weights, were evaluated zero-shot on 86 public tasks from 14 clinical domains in 20 languages, spanning examination questions and medical imaging. Tasks were assigned to six capability tiers before analysis. The primary outcome was the area under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (AUROC). Secondary outcomes included calibration, language effects and comparisons with published results and generative models. Across 179,111 cases, the mean AUROC of the best model per task was 0.94 for medical knowledge, 0.93 for text understanding, 0.83 for clinical judgement from text, 0.89 for image perception, 0.84 for 2D image findings and 0.69 for volumetric findings. Imaging tasks scored 0.09 (95% CI, 0.06 to 0.11) lower than text tasks (P < 0.001). The best model matched or exceeded the best published result on 13 of 26 text tasks and on 8 of 35 imaging tasks. Triage nurses outperformed the best model (accuracy, 0.85 vs. 0.56). One decision took 0.34 to 0.44 seconds and 24 to 81 joules on open-weight models run locally. Accuracy on identical questions fell by up to 39 percentage points from English to Yoruba. Generative models of the same family were 11 to 76 times slower, with 3 to 5 percentage points higher balanced accuracy. System 1 models decide quickly, cheaply and with usable confidence when the answer is stated in a text or recalled from medical knowledge, but not when it must be judged or read from an image.
  </details>

- **[OpenAttention: One Percent Is Enough with Tail Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30951)**  `arXiv:2610.30951`  
  _Hyunwoo Yu, Jongbeom Lee, Hyunbin Kim, Sungjun Park, Jaemin Choi, Jincheol Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention reads only a small subset of the key-value cache, and how to treat the unread tail remains a key challenge. Discarding the tail biases the output, and sampling it as previous approaches do fails at reads of 5% and below, where the tail likely holds heavy hitters the selector missed. Our key observation is that the simplest possible proxy, one token that folds the entire tail into its count-aware mean, already yields lower output error than a sample at every read behind a practical selector. We further show in closed form that at low reads sampling nearly doubles the weight error of discarding while a single proxy stays below it. Building on these findings, we explore approximating the dense attention distribution with a few proxies that target the tail. We propose OpenAttention, a pipeline of clustering, ErrorGate and top-k selection followed by a count-aware mean proxy. To implement clustering and selection both efficiently and effectively, we design training-free Query-Geometry Clustering (QGC) and a Query-Geometry Selector (QGS), which compress the keys while respecting the query directions. Additionally, unlike prior methods that rank clusters by centroid alone, ErrorGate allocates the exact read by the estimated weight error of each cluster. On RULER-32K HARD with Llama-3.1-8B, OpenAttention retains 99% of dense accuracy at a 1% read. It also achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across models, context lengths and datasets. Our customized attention kernel for OpenAttention runs the decode attention stage 10$\times$ faster than dense attention at a 1% read.
  </details>

- **[Foundations of the Scaling Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27789)**  `arXiv:2610.27789`  
  _Tianyu Zhao, Zhixiang Ren_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Empirical scaling laws relate loss to parameters $N$, training tokens $D$ and compute $C$. This technical note explains three mechanisms behind their exponents. Local approximation bounds function error at rate $N_e^{-\gamma s/d}$, with effective parameters $N_e$, smoothness $s$, reuse exponent $\gamma$ and dimension $d$; the loss function determines the risk bound. Independent feature coverage gives $\alpha_D=(\beta-1)/\zeta$ from predictive-weight decay $\beta$ and occurrence rarity $\zeta$. Separable-loss optimization determines training allocations and deployment crossovers. Nonlinear scoring can produce apparent emergent abilities, while grokking concerns delayed generalization and verification constrains test-time scaling. For regular molecular configurations, the $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ quotient gives $d_{\mathrm{int}}=d-6$ and relative approximation-exponent gain $6/d_{\mathrm{int}}$. Optimized dense-to-equivariant excess-loss ratios scale as $C^{p_{\mathrm{eq}}-p_{\mathrm{dense}}}$, where $p_j$ are compute-loss exponents and the prefactor depends on loss amplitudes and FLOP costs. Domain examples also cover visual resolution, conformational coverage and correlated-sample precision.
  </details>

- **[A Mixture-Gated Theory of Anisotropic Representation Dynamics in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23242)**  `arXiv:2610.23242`  
  _Nguyen Do, My T. Thai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small differences between internal representations can shrink or grow as they pass through a Transformer layer, affecting how information is preserved and how sensitive the model is to perturbations. Yet it remains unclear when changes in attention weights can turn a representation difference that would otherwise shrink into one that grows. We develop a Mixture-Gated Theory of Anisotropic Representation Dynamics that separates self-attention into value transport, query gating, and key gating. Our analysis reveals how the geometry of attended representations constrains gating and how interactions between these mechanisms can reverse contraction induced by value transport. We formalize this phenomenon as gating-induced regime switching, where a representation difference contracts under fixed attention routing but expands under full attention. We derive a necessary and sufficient spectral criterion that certifies the existence or absence of switching and identifies switching directions without exhaustive search. Beyond existence, our theory characterizes maximum relative switching strength, certifies the severity of contraction that gating can overcome, and determines the largest dimension of a subspace in which every nonzero direction switches. Experiments across four Transformer architectures validate these predictions through analytical and finite-forward comparisons, revealing distinct and joint query--key gating mechanisms and nontrivial switching subspaces. Our results provide a mechanistic and spectral understanding of how attention routing can alter local representation dynamics.
  </details>

- **[What Transfers from Training-Only Priors? Separating Knowledge, Ordinal Targets, and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11146)**  `arXiv:2610.11146`  
  _Qiye Lu, Jiang Ji, Liang Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-only teacher access lets a compact student deploy with local features and exported weights. This established distillation interface does not identify what transfers or when it helps. We separate label, query, capacity, and source-access budgets on three public credit archives and a previously unexamined bankruptcy archive. Shared tree parameters, shuffles, and optimizer interventions expose limitations of historical Ranking Prior Alignment; no tested single repair consistently rescues it. Heldout source queries and matched warmup and mappings retain a rank-listwise advantage over mapped staged KD in 24 credit configuration means and all six teacher--capacity means of a frozen new-archive test, with explicit query failures and stronger source-weight delivery under a different interface. A pretrained TabICLv2 follow-up at 100 labels initially improves matched supervision in only one of eight means; six of nine source-predicted policy gains reverse on target. A registered seen-archive intervention adds 500 or 2,000 unlabeled queries: all eight matched AUC means become positive under selected and fixed alpha. However, outer effects are mixed and no conditional paired-draw interval for these supervised contrasts lies wholly above zero. Increasing teacher context at 500 student labels does not beat matched supervision in any of eight ordinary-context means, despite stronger direct teacher performance. Rank KD retains substantial probability errors. Independent reloads verify zero guarded external inference accesses. Results support query/context boundary diagnostics, without universal superiority, population significance, a prospective transfer rule, or an LLM-specific advantage.
  </details>

- **[VETTA: Coordinating Turn- and Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08402)**  `arXiv:2610.08402`  
  _Jiaju Chen, Min Yang, Jinghua Piao, Xiaochong Lan, Xu Xia, Xiangnan He, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn LLM agents often receive sparse task feedback across several interactions, while generating each response token by token. This creates two related credit-assignment questions: which responses helped achieve the outcome, and which generation decisions mattered within each response? Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. These complementary limitations motivate learning credit at both levels and coordinating it in a single policy update. We introduce VETTA, a credit assignment method that jointly learns turn- and token-level values through separate heads on a shared lightweight critic. VETTA computes advantages along both temporal sequences and combines each turn advantage with a within-response-centered token residual for PPO updates. Furthermore, to reduce value-learning cost, the critic retains only early Transformer blocks from the pretrained checkpoint used to initialize the actor. Experiments on four agent benchmarks demonstrate VETTA's strong performance. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, VETTA achieves success rates of 95.5% on ALFWorld, 76.0% on WebShop, and 52.2% on ScienceWorld. With Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, it reaches 84.9% and 80.7% success on Sokoban under sparse and dense rewards, respectively. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient.
